R. 6(d) EXTENDS FOR ELECTRONIC SERVICE (mail, email, electronic, efiling_service_provider), the opposite of FRCP 6(d) since 2016 and wider than NC's mail-only 6(e). R. 33 HAS NO DEFENDANT FLOOR where R. 34 and R. 36 both give 45 days, so TWO later_of rows not three -- MA is NOT internally consistent the way VA is (21/21/21). Answer is 20 days. R. 12(a)(2)'s two limbs differ on whether the extension applies (notice of the COURT'S action vs SERVICE of a paper) and are separate rows for exactly that. PATRIOTS' DAY (third Monday in April) exists in no other jurisdiction here and is what the live verification used to prove the calendar is actually read: 20-day answer from 2026-03-31 rolls off Mon 2026-04-20 to 04-21. 56/56 in a new isolated suite, all 28 api/_lib suites pass, all 11 rule payloads and 6 holiday-year payloads run through the REAL endpoint validators. mass.gov 403s EVERY automated fetch including its own PDF download links while Playwright gets 200 -- a 403 there means "not a browser", never "page absent"; same shape as NC's nccourts.gov. 2026-08-25

- docs/SAIRN-OPEN-WORK-INDEX.md + LIVE DATA on LAW-PINNACLE-2026 (Hank) -- **THE 87/48 "DIVERGENCE" WAS A FALSE POSITIVE, AND CHASING IT FOUND A REAL LIVE DEFECT UNDERNEATH.** `sql/platform_reference_rules_divergence_2026-08-28.sql` reported 87 diverged rule ids and 48 diverged holiday ids between `LAW-PINNACLE-2026` and `LAW-TEST-2026`. Cause, found by diffing actual blobs: **`authority.verified_by`**, which `api/legal-deadlines.js` writes INTO `data` on every add_rule/add_holidays (`caller ? caller.employee_id : null`). TEST was loaded through an authenticated employee session -> `"hank-verify"` on every row; PINNACLE was backfilled by `tools/load_deadline_seed.py`, bearer-key only, no session -> `null` on every row. One field, present on 100% of rows by construction, so `md5(data::text)` differs on 100% of shared ids. The counts confirm it exactly: TEST holds 87 rules (live `rules_status`) and 8 jurisdictions x 6 years = 48 calendars. 87/87 and 48/48. This is precisely the false-positive class that file warns about at its own line 159 -- the warning was right and should stay.
  **NOT a customer-facing divergence either way:** `sql/sairnlaw_test_license_seed.sql` provisions LAW-TEST-2026 as `test@sairnlaw-verification.example`, an internal verification tenant deliberately left stale when PINNACLE became canonical 2026-08-25. Only two SAIRNlaw keys exist in `sql/`.
  **THE REAL FINDING: two committed fixes had never been loaded, and were live-wrong on the canonical customer licence.** `e1aa3f8` (Rule 6(d) does not reach Rule 4) and `a9daad1` (Florida exclusivity) changed SEED FILES. A seed-file change is inert until the loader runs, and nobody ran it. Live PINNACLE was computing FRCP 12(a)(1)(A)(i) on mailed service as **2027-06-25 (+3)** and 12(a)(2) as **2027-08-05 (+3)** -- the exact three-days-late defect `e1aa3f8`'s own message says it fixed. Reloaded us_federal + florida + illinois into PINNACLE on Michael's go-ahead: 23 rules + 2 calendars, zero failures. **Verified by changed OUTCOME on identical inputs, not the loader's exit code:** 12(a)(1)(A)(i) -> **2027-06-22**, 12(a)(2) -> **2027-08-02**, both `service_extension.state:"not_requested"`; Florida `applied` -> **`applied_exclusivity_assumed`** (date unchanged by design). **Mirror-image control in the same pass:** `frcp-12a1B-counterclaim-crossclaim` KEEPS its +3 -> 2027-06-25, so Rule 5 rows were not stripped along with the Rule 4 ones.
  **CAVEAT CLOSED, not hand-waved.** `verified_by` explains why all 87 flagged, not that the other 84 blobs agreed. Ran a read-only compute-diff of all 89 repo rules in TEST's 8 jurisdictions on BOTH licences, 178 probes, comparing whole normalised responses with `verified_by` stripped: **160 identical with a real date, 4 identical refusals, 4 probes for the 2 rule ids TEST never had (`frcp-15a3-response-to-amended-pleading`, `oh-appr-4B1-cross-appeal`), 10 divergent across exactly 7 rule ids -- every one of them a rule touched by the two 2026-08-27 commits, with TEST the stale side.** Only TWO produce a different DATE: `frcp-12a1Ai` and `frcp-12a2`, both +3 on mail. The other five differ in note/label text only. So: 80 of 87 shared ids are byte-equivalent modulo `verified_by`, 7 are stale-vs-corrected, 0 are unexplained. **Re-ran the diff before trusting it** -- the first pass showed an 11th divergence on `ca-ccp-41220a3-response-to-summons` that was a transient `Upstream connection error` on one side, not a data difference; a single-pass result would have reported a phantom California defect.
  **SECOND STALE-LABEL PROBLEM, corrected in the index:** it claimed 24 computing jurisdictions / 269 computing rules live. Live is **19 / 226** (`rules_status`, re-verified after the reload). The five "so the live count is now ..." updates (UT, NV, OR, OK, SC) each asserted a LIVE number for a jurisdiction written to a seed file and loaded to no licence -- 43 computing rules exist only in `sql/`, plus `ct` which is unprovisioned on purpose. There is no third tenant they could have landed in. The header cell of that row said 19/226 all along and was the only thing in it that was right. **Loading those five was NOT done -- out of scope for tonight's authorisation, and it is new coverage rather than a defect fix. Queued.**
  **Standing lesson worth more than the fix: "fix committed" != "fix live" for anything whose payload is a seed file.** The push protocol's live-verify step covers deployed CODE; rule DATA has no equivalent gate, which is exactly how this survived a day on the canonical licence.

- api/legal-deadlines.js + tools/sairn_load_state_check.py + LIVE DATA (Hank) -- **THREE QUEUED ITEMS CLOSED, AND THE GATE FOUND A SIXTH THING ON ITS FIRST RUN.**
  **(1) UT/NV/OR/OK/SC LOADED into LAW-PINNACLE-2026** on Michael's authorisation -- 43 rules + 25 calendars, zero failures, so live coverage is now **24 computing jurisdictions / 269 computing rules**, which is what the index had been claiming since 2026-08-27 and is now actually true. Verified in TWO passes, because "every rule returns a date" and "the calendar is actually read" are different claims and only the second fails quietly. Pass 1: 43/43 compute a real date. Pass 2, with the trigger date derived from each rule's own count so the base period lands exactly on the target holiday: **Pioneer Day (ut, 2026-07-24) rolls, Nevada Day (nv, 2026-10-30) rolls, Confederate Memorial Day (sc, 2026-05-11) rolls** -- none of the three exists in any other jurisdiction, so nothing else could produce those dates. **Two NEGATIVE probes, which are the ones a copied allowlist would fail:** Oklahoma OMITS Juneteenth and Oregon OMITS Columbus Day, so 2026-06-19 (ok) and 2026-10-12 (or) must NOT roll, and they do not. Utah's 2027 cap still refuses `NOT_PROVISIONED` -- correct, its calendar is 2026 only.
  **(2) DIVERGENCE QUERY FIXED** -- all three hash sites now `md5((data::jsonb #- '{authority,verified_by}')::text)`. jsonb cast is deliberate: `#-` has no json counterpart. Expected on re-run: 7 diverged rules, 0 calendars. **NOT RUN by me -- no DB access from this clone; it needs a Supabase-editor run to confirm the 7/0.**
  **(3) THE LOAD-STATE GATE, built and PROVEN AGAINST THE DEFECT IT EXISTS FOR.** New read-only `rules_fingerprint` action returns `{entry_id, version, hash}` per row -- a hash, not the blobs, so it detects drift without turning a licence-gated endpoint into a bulk export of every rule's content. `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py` diffs seeds vs licence: MISSING / STALE / EXTRA, exit 1 on drift, **exit 2 when it could not tell, so "unreachable" can never be misread as "pass"**. It does NOT offer to fix what it finds -- reloading a customer licence is a decision with a person's name on it.
  `version` is REPORTED, never trusted: where a row is stale while its version matches the seed's, it says so under its own heading, which is the version discipline failing out loud.
  **Retroactive proof, not a claim:** run against LAW-TEST-2026 (still holding the pre-fix rows) it names **exactly the same 7 stale rule ids** the compute-diff found earlier tonight -- by content hash, a completely different method from live computes -- and fires the version-silent warning on all 7. Against PINNACLE it now reports **0 missing / 0 stale / 0 extra**. `--expect-stale` exits 0 for a knowingly-behind tenant so a known-stale licence never trains anyone to ignore red.
  **THE TWO HASH IMPLEMENTATIONS WERE PROVEN EQUAL, NOT ASSUMED** -- JS extracted from the endpoint source and run against the Python over all 401 seed entries: 401/401 identical. A checker whose digest disagrees with the server's invents drift and gets switched off within a week.
  **THAT CROSS-CHECK FAILED FIRST TIME, AND THE FAILURE WAS REAL: FIVE 2027 CALENDARS ARE DEFINED TWICE WITH DIFFERENT CONTENT.** `in:2027 mi:2027 oh:2027 pa:2027 us-federal:2027` each appear in BOTH `calendars_2027_2031.json` and their own `seed_*.json`. The 2027_2031 copies carry **2027-12-31 "New Year's Day (observed)"** -- 1 Jan 2028 is a Saturday -- and the per-state copies omit it. Whichever the loader is handed LAST wins, decided by argument order, not by anything in the repo. **CONFIRMED LIVE: PINNACLE holds the copies WITHOUT it** -- federal 21-day answer triggered 2027-12-10 returns **2027-12-31 with no rollover step**. Error direction is EARLY (conservative), not late, which is why this is a question and not an emergency. **NOT resolved by me: which copy is canonical is a sourcing question about FRCP 6(a)(6), and picking one silently is exactly what this gate exists to prevent. Michael's call.**
  **CONNECTICUT no longer reads as MISSING forever.** Its seed now carries a top-level `_hold_reason`; the checker holds those entries out of the comparison and lists them separately. The loader reads only `rules` and `holiday_calendars`, so the key records intent without changing what a load does. A check that is always red is a check nobody reads.
  779/779 deadline tests pass, unchanged -- this adds an action and a tool and touches no computation.

- sql/sairnlaw_deadline_calendars_2027_2031.json + five seed files + tools/ (Hank) -- **2027-12-31 RESOLVED FROM PRIMARY SOURCE, AND IT WAS NOT "TAKE THE MORE COMPLETE FILE".** Five 2027 calendars were defined twice with different content. Reading each jurisdiction's own authority rather than picking a winner produced **four yeses and one no**:
  **us-federal YES.** FRCP 6(a)(6)(A) defines a legal holiday as "the day set aside **by statute** for observing New Year's Day"; 5 U.S.C. 6103(b)(1) provides that "instead of a holiday that occurs on a Saturday, the Friday immediately before is a legal public holiday". 1 Jan 2028 is a Saturday, so Friday 2027-12-31 is that day. **The deciding evidence was internal consistency, not completeness:** the seed copy already applied the SAME 6103(b) mechanism to Christmas (25 Dec 2027 is a Saturday; it carried 2027-12-24 "Christmas Day (observed)") and omitted the identical shift four weeks later. No reading of 6(a)(6) makes 12-24 a holiday and 12-31 not. **Limit recorded, not argued away:** 6103(b)(1) is written "for employees whose basic workweek is Monday through Friday" and for 5 U.S.C. 6309, not as a redesignation for all purposes.
  **in YES** -- IC 1-1-9-1(b)'s two-way shift, already applied by the same file to 2027-07-05 and 2027-12-24. **mi YES, and it is the subtle one** -- MCR 8.110(D)(2) shifts Saturday holidays back to Friday, so 12-31 is New Year's Day observed and sits ALONGSIDE New Year's Eve, which itself falls on Friday 12-31 and shifts back to Thursday 12-30. Two adjacent holidays is correct, and is the exact shape the file already carried for 12-23/12-24. **pa YES by the FEDERAL half** of Pa.R.J.A. 107(b)'s two-sovereign construction, mirroring its existing federal-observed 12-24 alongside statutory 12-25.
  **oh NO.** Ohio Rev. Code 1.14 does not shift Saturday holidays -- which is why oh has no 2027-06-18 and no 2027-12-24 where federal has both. **The 2027_2031 file's DATES were right for Ohio and its NOTE was wrong:** a generic amendment note applied to all five jurisdictions asserted "this jurisdiction observes it on FRIDAY 2027-12-31", contradicting its own date list. That is the dangerous direction -- a reader checking the reasoning would have "corrected" the data to match the prose.
  **FIXED STRUCTURALLY, not just for this round:** the duplicate 2027 blocks were removed from all five seed files by TEXT SPLICE (219 pure deletions, zero reformatting -- three of the five do not round-trip through json.dumps and a re-dump would have buried the change), leaving `calendars_2027_2031.json` as the single definition. Its five generic notes were replaced with per-jurisdiction reasoning. 131 calendar entries, 131 distinct, **0 duplicates**.
  **LIVE-VERIFIED BY CHANGED OUTCOME on identical inputs, with the negative control in the same pass.** Before: all five returned 2027-12-31 with `steps:['base_period']`. After: **us-federal / in / mi / pa -> 2028-01-03 with a `rollover` step**, and **oh -> 2027-12-31, still no rollover** -- which proves the reload did not blanket-apply the holiday and that Ohio's non-shift decision actually landed.
  **GATES CONSOLIDATED to one.** `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py` is canonical; `tools/sairnlaw_build_load_gate.py` + `sql/sairnlaw_load_gate_generated.sql` (`ca83d1f`, the parallel session's DB-side SQL gate) are removed. Reasons in the tool's own docstring so the deleted work is not silently erased: mine needs only a licence key (runs from any clone, can gate a pre-push step), reads the seeds at run time so it cannot go stale where a GENERATED gate must be regenerated after every seed edit, covers CALENDARS as well as rules -- theirs globbed `seed_*.json` only and **would have missed tonight's five-calendar finding entirely** -- and reports MISSING/EXTRA, not just STALE. What theirs had that mine does not: Postgres compares jsonb canonically, so it never had to make two languages agree on a digest; that risk here was closed by proof, 401/401 entries identical. **A defect in the deleted gate, recorded so it is not reintroduced: its INERT_KEYS listed `computation` as unable to change a date.** It selects the counting standard, and a rule whose standard drifted would have passed that gate clean.
  Gate now exits 0 on PINNACLE: 269 rules, 131 calendars, 0 missing / 0 stale / 0 extra. 779/779 deadline tests pass.

- api/reference-fingerprint.js + tools/sairn_load_state_check.py (Hank) -- **ONE GATE STYLE PLATFORM-WIDE, and it found a real gap on its first run against a new app.** New read-only `api/reference-fingerprint.js` covers every app's per-licence reference tables; `tools/deadline_load_state_check.py` renamed to `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py` and given `--app`. **Hashes the WHOLE ROW, not `data`** -- only SAIRNlaw keeps everything in one blob; `rf_contingency_rules` keeps `count`/`unit` as real columns, so a `data`-only fingerprint would miss a wrong count entirely (CC's observation from the superseded gate, carried over rather than rediscovered). **Null-valued keys dropped on BOTH sides**, which is load-bearing: `effective_to`/`role`/`facility_class`/`business_day_basis` come back from Postgres as explicit nulls and every such row would otherwise read as drifted forever. Also carried over: the two write-path normalisations read out of `api/sd-data.js` (`state` uppercased, `status` defaults to `active`) -- without them every `alf_payer_rules` row would have reported false STALE on the first run.
  **LIVE RESULTS, all four tables verified in one pass:** sairncare `alf_compliance_rules` 16/16 and `alf_payer_rules` 6/6 clean (ALF-TEST-2026); sairndental `dnt_cred_rules` 6/6 clean (DNT-PINNACLE-2026); sairnroofing `rf_cert_rules` 3/3 clean. **Zero false positives anywhere** -- the normalisations are right.
  **THE FINDING: `rf_contingency_rules` holds 0 rows on RF-PINNACLE-2026 while the seed has 2** (`RFCON-OH-HSSA-CANCEL`, `RFCON-OH-HSSA-SCOPE`). Same class as tonight's SAIRNlaw defect -- seeded in the repo, never loaded live. **It does not fail loudly:** `api/sd-data.js:4033` returns `{ok:true, data:[], provisioned:true}`, so a foreman asking for the Ohio HSSA cancellation window gets an empty list with no signal that anything is missing. **NOT LOADED BY ME** -- writes to this table require a real management session token for RF-PINNACLE-2026, which is a materially different operation from the SAIRNlaw bearer-key loader, and it is a different app than tonight's authorisation covered. Raised for Michael. I did NOT trace what the roofing UI renders on an empty list, so no claim is made about what a contractor actually sees.
  **CC FLAGGED IN THE FILE THEY WOULD REACH FOR:** `tools/sairn_build_load_gates.py` now opens with a superseded header addressed to them, explaining that a GENERATED gate must be regenerated after every seed edit and a forgotten regeneration is the same silent-failure shape the gate exists to catch. The file is KEPT, not deleted, because three of its findings are load-bearing and are now carried in the replacement -- promoted columns, the two normalisations, and that `sc_anesthesia_base_units` has NO seed file anywhere in the repo, so no gate can be built for it and that absence is itself the finding. Its five generated `.sql` gates are removed; regenerating them would put two gates back.
  **NEW REPORT the old gate could not produce:** seed keys that NO column can store. The write paths persist an explicit column list and discard anything else, so a seed field nobody stores is invisible from both ends. Underscore-prefixed keys are excluded by convention (`_note` is documentation and is meant not to load).
  SAIRNlaw unchanged after the rename: 269 rules, 131 calendars, 0/0/0.

- tools/seed_load_gate_hook.py + .claude/settings.json + CLAUDE.md (Hank) -- **ITEM 4 DONE: the gate runs on every push instead of from memory.** PreToolUse Bash hook, same shape as `git_push_master_guard.py`. Reads the commits ACTUALLY being pushed and returns immediately if none touch a seed file, so an ordinary push costs nothing. Live matches repo -> allow silently (the normal case when you loaded first, which is why a correct workflow feels no friction). Drift -> **DENY**, naming app, rule id and reload command. Could-not-tell (no key, endpoint down) -> allow with a loud note saying **it is not a pass** -- blocking someone with no licence key would get the hook deleted within a day, and a deleted hook checks nothing.
  **PROVEN TO BLOCK BEFORE BEING TRUSTED**, same standard as the federal service-extension test: a temp commit changing one Florida citation string was made, the hook denied it naming `fl-rcp-1140a1-answer-to-complaint seed 553d2ae906e3903a != live c9abcfec7547239e`, `SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off` was confirmed to allow, the temp commit was reset, and the hook confirmed silent again on the clean tree. A gate nobody has seen fire is a gate whose behaviour nobody knows.
  **Clone-agnostic by construction** -- repo comes from `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` against the CWD, never a fixed path; with four clones a hardcoded path checks the wrong tree three times out of four. Fails OPEN on internal error, same standard as the other hooks. The one thing that CAN go stale is named in its own header: `SEED_PATTERNS` matches path shapes so new seed files are covered automatically, but a new APP must be added by hand. CLAUDE.md gains step 3 of the Push Protocol.
  **DIVERGENCE SWEEP CONFIRMED by Michael 2026-08-29: 7 rules, 5 calendars, everything else consistent. I predicted 7 and 0.** The 5 are `in/mi/oh/pa/us-federal :2027`, corrected on PINNACLE *later the same day* the 0 was written, with TEST still holding the pre-correction copies. Recorded in the SQL file WITH the reason rather than editing the note to match the result -- a prediction written before a change and read after it is a prediction about a different database.
  **ITEM 2 RESOLVED BY NOT DOING IT, which is the correct answer.** `sc_anesthesia_base_units` was carried in CC's gate as "no seed file anywhere in the repo -- the absence is the finding", and **I repeated that twice before checking it. It is wrong in both halves.** SAIRNcode's own build record (`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-cc.md`, 2026-08-20) says the table is **empty-by-default and Source-required**: the coder enters base units with their own citation, the case form auto-fills only from a verified match, and says "not in your reference table yet" rather than inventing a number. It is **customer-owned data, correctly seedless** -- and its migration is queued and unrun, so there is no live table to compare against either. **Authoring a seed would have pushed unverified values into a table built to hold only what the practice verified -- the fabricated-data class, committed by the tool meant to prevent it.** Corrected in both files. General shape worth keeping: before calling a missing seed a defect, ask whether the table is platform reference content or the customer's own; only the first kind can drift, because only the first kind has a repo to drift from.
  **ITEM 1 BLOCKED, and not probed.** `rf_contingency_rules` writes need a MANAGEMENT session on RF-PINNACLE-2026 (`api/sd-data.js:4043`); there is no bearer-key-only path. `check_license` confirms the licence is live and active; `whoami` returns `NO_SESSION`. **I did not probe with `bootstrap`** -- it is a WRITE that creates a real owner credential when a licence is unprovisioned, which is exactly the mistake `tools/load_deadline_seed.py`'s own header records ("NEVER PROBE A DEPLOYMENT WITH A WRITE ENDPOINT" -- a junk `zz`/`__probe__` row is still in the live deadline store). So either the licence already has credentials and I would be guessing a PIN against a real lockout threshold, or it does not and bootstrapping is a provisioning decision rather than a data load. Both need Michael. Same discipline that produced `sql/sairnlaw_test_license_seed.sql`.

- LIVE DATA on RF-PINNACLE-2026 + docs/SAIRN-OPEN-WORK-INDEX.md (Hank) -- **ITEM 1 CLOSED, and reading the roster first changed the answer twice.** Michael's recollection was that `rf-verify-admin` had been seeded and later CLEANED UP. **It has not been.** `sql/sairnroofing_access_panel_verify_cleanup_2026-08-28.sql` deletes all five disposable accounts and **has not been run** -- every one is still live. Checked before touching anything, exactly as instructed.
  **THE ROSTER CONTRADICTED THE OPEN-WORK INDEX TOO.** Row 186 was HIGH PRIORITY on the basis that RF-PINNACLE-2026 had **zero active owners** and was API-unrecoverable. Live `roster`, read with a real signed session: 5 rows and **TWO active owners** -- `rf-verify-admin` is **role `owner`, not the `admin` its own seed file still says** (someone applied precisely the promotion that row proposed as recovery action 1), and `rf-verify-owner` is **`active: true`** where the row recorded it false. `rf-verify-ui` is still owner/inactive; both foremen active. So the licence recovered at some point and nothing said so. Row corrected; **the four-app trapdoor ANALYSIS underneath it is untouched and still open** -- that is about the missing recovery PATH, not this licence's state.
  **NOTED, because it is the next trapdoor rather than a tidy-up:** the recovery now rests on a credential that a queued cleanup script is written to delete. Running that file as-is would recreate the exact zero-owner state row 186 describes. It should not be run until an owner that survives it exists.
  **No bootstrap decision was needed and none was made.** `MANAGEMENT_ROLES = {owner, admin}` (`api/rf-auth.js:95`) is what gates `rf_contingency_rules` writes, so the existing owner session sufficed. I logged in with the PIN already documented in `sql/sairnroofing_verify_admin_seed.sql` -- a recorded credential, not a guess -- and still did **not** probe with `bootstrap`, which is a WRITE.
  **LOADED AND VERIFIED THROUGH A SECOND PATH.** Before: `rf_contingency_rules` 0 rows, `provisioned: true` (so it was failing silently, not refusing). Wrote both Ohio HSSA rules -- `RFCON-OH-HSSA-CANCEL` and `RFCON-OH-HSSA-SCOPE`, both `OH / execution / 3 business_days / basis oh_hssa`. After: 2 rows, read back correct. **Then confirmed independently by the load-state gate, which recomputes the content hash from the seed rather than trusting the write's own echo: `rf_contingency_rules` 2/2, `rf_cert_rules` 3/3, 0 missing / 0 stale / 0 extra, exit 0.** SAIRNroofing is now clean on both tables.
  Provenance note: `verified_by` on both rows is `rf-verify-admin`, a disposable account, because the server stamps it from the session. Acceptable on a demo licence, wrong on a real one -- worth remembering before this pattern is used to seed a paying customer.

- sql/sairnroofing_access_panel_verify_cleanup_2026-08-28.sql + docs/2026-08-29-single-provisioning-role-trapdoor-analysis.md + SAIRN-BACKLOG.md (Hank) -- **CLEANUP REWRITTEN AND GUARDED; TRAPDOOR ANALYSED; `verified_by` LOGGED.**
  **The cleanup file: narrowed to the two foremen per Michael, and made structurally safe.** It now deletes only `rf-verify-fmA`/`fmB` -- neither is an owner, so it cannot change the active-owner count at all. Added a `do $$` guard that asserts the END STATE is one of the two safe shapes (zero rows, or >=1 active provisioner) and **rolls the whole transaction back otherwise**, so a future pass that widens the delete list cannot leave the licence unrecoverable whether or not it read the reasoning.
  **ONE CORRECTION RAISED BEFORE ACTING, and it is worth keeping.** The concern was that deleting `rf-verify-owner` recreates the zero-owner lockout. **Deleting ALL FIVE would not have** -- the trapdoor is "rows exist AND none is an active provisioner", because `bootstrap` refuses 409 while ANY row exists and does not filter on `active`. Zero rows RE-ARMS bootstrap and is recovery, which is what the original file's reasoning was built on. **The only dangerous shape is deleting SOME owners and leaving others**, which is exactly how this licence got stuck. Proceeded with the narrower instruction anyway -- it is safe under either reading -- and wrote the distinction into the file so nobody has to hold it in their head again. **Cost of the conservative option, stated in the file rather than hidden: all three owner rows survive and two are loggable-in with PINs committed to this repo.**
  **NOT RUN -- no DB access from this clone** (re-verified: no Supabase env vars, `.env.local` is 0 bytes, no `psql`), and no roofing table grants DELETE to `service_role`, so the API cannot perform it by design. Needs a Supabase-editor run.
  **THE TRAPDOOR ANALYSIS, and the finding that changes its recommendation.** Read from source, not carried from the index row. `PROVISIONING_ROLES`: dnt `['owner']`, mech `['owner']`, rf `['owner']`, sc `['admin']`, sd `['owner','admin']`. **SAIRNdental is the worst-placed, not SAIRNcode** -- the only app where PROVISIONING and MANAGEMENT are both a single role AND the same role, so there is not even an `admin` to promote. The SAIRNcode `admin`-not-`owner` trap is real but applies to NEW code only: all five existing guards read `PROVISIONING_ROLES` rather than a literal, which I checked rather than assumed.
  **THE API CANNOT REACH THE STATE.** `set_active` requires an active provisioning caller, re-reads the roster to confirm the caller's OWN row is still active, refuses self-deactivation, and refuses when the target is the last active provisioner. The count cannot cross 1 -> 0 by any API path. So RF-PINNACLE-2026 got stuck through **seed files and direct SQL**, which is the only door and the one with nothing on it. **That kills the obvious fix and also the usual objection to it:** softening `bootstrap` to "no ACTIVE provisioner" is normally rejected because someone with the licence key could deactivate their way to a fresh owner -- **that attack is not reachable**. It is still the wrong fix, for a different reason: a licence key is shared with every employee and is not a secret, so an org legitimately sitting at zero active provisioners (owner on leave) would let anyone holding the key seize a live tenant. Recommendation: **guard the SQL door (urgent), add detection reading each app's own PROVISIONING_ROLES (RF sat in the state, recovered, and nothing noticed either transition -- the index row still asserted zero active owners when the live roster had two), and treat widening the role pool as a per-app authorisation decision rather than a safety patch** -- StoneDesk's two roles are a reduction in exposure, not immunity, so the real question is "how many provisioners must remain active".
  **`verified_by` logged in SAIRN-BACKLOG.md as a pattern, not a design.** It records who was SIGNED IN when a row was written, not who verified the content, while its schema comments claim the latter -- and the real provenance is already in `data.authority`. It has cost once already in the other direction: being inside the SAIRNlaw `data` blob is what produced the false 87/48 divergence, and two tools now subtract it explicitly. Seven tables affected. Acceptable on demo tenants; the line not to cross is a paying customer, and nothing currently stops it.

- tools/employee_auth_guard_check.py + api/provisioner-health.js + tools/licence_recoverability_check.py + docs/2026-08-29-sairndental-provisioning-role-decision.md (Hank) -- **THE SQL DOOR IS GUARDED, THE STATE IS DETECTED, AND THE SAIRNDENTAL DECISION IS WRITTEN UP.**
  **GUARD (top priority).** Five 2026-08-28 Access-panel cleanups now end in a `do $$` block asserting the END STATE and rolling the whole transaction back otherwise: **safe shape A = zero rows** (this RE-ARMS `bootstrap`, it is recovery not lockout), **safe shape B = >=1 active provisioner**. Each file uses **its own app's roles read from source** -- sc `['admin']`, dnt/mech/rf `['owner']`, sd `['owner','admin']`; a guard hardcoding `owner` would pass SAIRNcode clean forever while checking nothing. `tools/employee_auth_guard_check.py` requires BOTH a transaction and the guard on any `sql/` file writing an `*_employee_auth` table -- a guard with no transaction cannot roll back, which is why the older files could not just have one pasted in. **19 pre-existing writers are GRANDFATHERED in an explicit list, not silently excluded; they are not fixed, only visible.** 24 writers / 5 guarded / 19 grandfathered / 0 unguarded. Wired as check 2 of the push hook (renamed `seed_load_gate_hook.py` -> `sairn_push_gate_hook.py`), and **proven to block before being trusted**: a temp unguarded delete against `sairndental_employee_auth` was committed and the hook denied the push naming the file and both reasons.
  **DETECTION.** `api/provisioner-health.js` -- read-only, licence-key only, returns COUNTS and never an employee id. **Deliberately not a `roster` call: roster needs a management session, and a licence in the trapdoor has nobody who can sign in, so that check would be unavailable exactly when it matters.** The five auth modules now **export their own `PROVISIONING_ROLES` and table name** (one additive line each, `node --check` clean, verified by loading all five and printing them) so the detector cannot drift from the rule it checks. Three states, and `NO_CREDENTIALS` is deliberately NOT folded into the failure count; an app with no `set_active` returns `NOT_APPLICABLE` rather than `HEALTHY`, so a future app that adds it cannot inherit a clean answer it never earned. **LIVE SWEEP OF ALL SEVEN LICENCES ACROSS FIVE APPS: every one HEALTHY.** Two things that sweep surfaced: **SD-AUDIT-2026 is HEALTHY (4 rows / 2 active provisioners)** despite `api/sd-auth.js:304-308` naming it as the licence that was LOST to this exact trapdoor -- it has recovered and the comment is stale; and **dnt, mech and sc each sit at exactly ONE active provisioner**, which the API cannot reduce but any SQL touching those rows can.
  **SAIRNDENTAL DECISION DOC written, nothing built.** Five options with costs and reversibility. **The finding that shapes it: SAIRNdental has NO `admin` role.** Its vocabulary is `owner, frontdesk, provider`, yet `api/dnt-auth.js:146` says "'admin' runs the office but does not mint identities" -- reasoning carried over from an app that HAS one, making today's design look like a deliberate narrowing from two roles to one when it was never two. That comment should be fixed under any option. **Recommendation: Option A (leave it) plus the comment fix, with Option D (a new `manager` role, matching mech/roofing's `admin`) as the thing to build when a customer actually asks.** The reasoning that decides it: **widening was proposed as a lockout mitigation and is not one** -- the API could never reach the trapdoor, so the real problem widening solves is "owner unavailable, nobody can offboard staff", which no customer has reported and which B/C guess at. B and C are cheap to do and expensive to undo; D is the reverse and matches the platform's existing shape. **Option E (break-glass) cannot be built safely today** -- a licence key is shared with every employee and is not a secret, so recovery gated on it alone lets anyone holding it seize a tenant, and SAIRNdental has no verified-email flow.
  **A MISTAKE, recorded because it cost real work:** I ran `git reset --hard HEAD~1` to remove a deliberate proof commit while unrelated UNCOMMITTED work sat in the same tree, and it destroyed the guard insertions and the hook edits. Redone from the scratchpad script. Earlier the same night I stashed before rebasing for exactly this reason. **Commit or stash before any hard reset, including one that looks like it only touches a commit you just made.**
  Also noted: the post-push deploy hook flagged `sairn.vercel.app/stonedesk` as not matching HEAD. **I have not touched `stonedesk.html` this session** and both new endpoints (`reference-fingerprint`, `provisioner-health`) deployed and answered within one poll, so I treated it as a false positive rather than pushing a re-trigger commit for a file I never changed.

- sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_arkansas.json + calendars + api/_lib/deadline-engine.js + docs (Hank) -- **ARKANSAS SEEDED. The gate had it BLOCKED, and the thing that cleared it was a channel the gate itself had named and never tried.**
  **THE BLOCK WAS REAL AND IS STILL TRUE.** Ark. Code Ann. 1-5-101 -- specifically subsection (b), the observance shift -- has never been read on a primary source. Four secondary publishers agree on the sentence and the gate refused to seed on agreement. **What changed:** every earlier attempt went to `media.ark.org/artreasury/` (2026 and 2027 both 403). The **SECRETARY OF STATE** publishes the same sheet -- `sos.arkansas.gov/uploads/holidays_2026.pdf`, HTTP 200, 204,182 B, real text via pypdf, headed *"2026 State Holidays. Legal holidays by authority of Act 304 of 2001 and Amended by Act 561 of 2017."* Sec. 1b of the gate had listed exactly this as the third of three routes that would clear it: *"a court or agency document that QUOTES (b) rather than citing 1-5-101 -- the Treasurer's sheet was the near miss."*
  **THE DISTINCTION THAT MAKES THE SEED HONEST, and it is narrow: the calendar is TRANSCRIBED, not DERIVED.** A calendar in this engine is an enumerated list of dates, not a shift rule, so every 2026 date now comes off a primary state publication and no unread statute is relied on. The gate blocked a *generated* calendar spanning years; it does not block a *transcribed* one for a year the State has published. **2027 REFUSES `NOT_PROVISIONED`** -- that sheet 404s and deriving it would need subsection (b). Utah's cap shape, and the safe direction. Verified live.
  **THE SHEET CONFIRMED BOTH TRAPS THE GATE PREDICTED:** Christmas Eve IS on the state list (no other seeded state except South Carolina has 24 December) and **Juneteenth and Columbus Day are NOT** -- so the state-federal union ARCP 6(a) requires is mandatory, and a state-only calendar computes EARLY two days a year. It also SHOWS the Saturday limb firing in the one 2026 instance where it does: *"July 4 . . . (Observed on Friday, July 3, 2026)"*. No 2026 holiday falls on a Sunday, so the unproven half of (b) never fires in what was seeded.
  **13 RULES, all read verbatim from the court's own PDF** (`opinions.arcourts.gov`, 1,311,020 B, 195pp, *"current through June 4, 2026"*). New engine standards: **`ar_rcp_6` with a FOURTEEN-DAY short-period exclusion -- the longest on the platform** (six states use 7, three use 11, three have none; copying any of them computes EARLY on the 10-day and 5-day rows), and **`ar_rcp_6_d`, three BUSINESS days with E-SERVICE EXPRESSLY INCLUDED** -- the opposite of the federal rule, Nevada, WV and NY. **Do not reason from FRCP 6(a) here:** the federal rule abolished its exclusion in 2009 and Arkansas went the other way.
  **ARKANSAS SETTLES THE RULE 4 / RULE 5 QUESTION IN ITS OWN TEXT.** ARCP 6(d)'s proviso names Rule 4 outright, which is direct textual confirmation of the reading two federal rows had to be corrected to on 2026-08-27 by inference. Both halves are encoded and asserted: the answer rows take NO extension, the cross-claim row KEEPS three business days.
  **ENGINE PUSHED FIRST, ON ITS OWN, THEN LOADED** -- the loader's standing warning, and the reason NC's 13 rules were once rejected wholesale. 13 rules + 1 calendar loaded, zero failures. **20/20 LIVE ASSERTIONS PASSED** on LAW-PINNACLE-2026, including the carve-out both ways, the 14-day exclusion as arithmetic (5-day reply skipping Labor Day), e-mail extending where personal service does not, Juneteenth excluded as an intermediate day, Christmas Eve rolling through Christmas and the weekend, a 60-day incarcerated-defendant period no other jurisdiction has, the first BACKWARD row outside the federal seed, and the 2027 refusal.
  **A NEAR-MISS WORTH KEEPING: `effective_from` is PER RULE, not the collection's currency date.** The first draft used 2026-06-04 for all 13 rows and every Rule 12 row then refused `NO_RULE_IN_FORCE` for triggers before June 2026 -- against rules that were in force. Each rule's own HISTORY line has the real answer: Rules 6/33/34/36 were *"amended and effective June 4, 2026"*, but **Rule 12's current text dates from *"amended June 21, 2018, effective January 1, 2019"***. A currency date and an effective date are different things; the engine computes against the law as it stood at the TRIGGER date and made the difference visible immediately. Asserted in the test file both ways.
  **NINE EXISTING TEST FILES BROKE AND THAT WAS THE POINT** -- each carries a standing invariant asserting the EXACT set of jurisdictions declaring a coverage entry, so adding `ar` forced the question rather than letting it slide. Massachusetts needed a second edit its siblings did not: it also checks each summary NAMES ITS OWN jurisdiction, and the regex map had no `ar` key, so it threw instead of failing an assertion.
  Live coverage is now **25 computing jurisdictions / 282 computing rules**, verified by the load-state gate (282/282 rules, 132/132 calendars, 0/0/0) rather than by counting seed files. **816/816 deadline tests pass** (779 + 37 new).

- sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_alabama.json + calendars + engine + docs (Hank) -- **ALABAMA SEEDED (11 rules, 16/16 live), AND WISCONSIN IS BLOCKED ON A DECISION I FOUND THE EVIDENCE FOR BUT DID NOT MAKE.**
  **ALABAMA.** Live is now **26 computing jurisdictions / 293 computing rules**, verified by the load-state gate (293/293 rules, 133/133 calendars, 0/0/0). All four of the gate's "would be wrong if inherited" items encoded AND asserted: **(1) the service order is FEDERAL after-expiry** -- "3 days are added AFTER THE PERIOD WOULD OTHERWISE EXPIRE" -- and the test asserts against `2026-06-03`, the date period-lengthening would have produced, since nearly every recently seeded state uses that other order; **(2) the exclusion is ELEVEN**, not 7 and not Arkansas's 14; **(3) the holidays are a THREE-SOURCE union** because Ala. R. Civ. P. 6(a)(4) is the only seeded rule that NAMES ITS OWN eleven days and then sweeps in both the federal list and Ala. Code 1-3-8; **(4) Thanksgiving has NO statutory date** ("as designated by the Governor") so it comes from the federal limb, recorded rather than assumed.
  **TWO DATES EXIST ON NO OTHER CALENDAR IN THIS PLATFORM** -- Confederate Memorial Day (4th Mon April) and Jefferson Davis' Birthday (1st Mon June). Neither is federal and neither is in the rule's own eleven; both reach deadlines only through 6(a)(4)(B), and both are asserted as arithmetic. The answer row from 2026-05-01 rolls TWICE -- Sunday, then Jefferson Davis' Birthday -- which nothing else here can produce.
  **A FOURTH TRAP CAUGHT IN THE RULE TEXT, not in the gate: "MAY" vs "SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED".** Rules 33 and 34 say a defendant MAY serve within 45 days -- an ELECTION -- while Rule 36 says a defendant SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED to serve before 45 days, a mandatory FLOOR. Only 36 is a `resolve_periods later_of`; encoding the elections as floors would report dates the rules do not guarantee. Same distinction South Carolina's Rule 36 row records, and asserted here by showing 33 and 34 land on the plain 30-day date on identical facts.
  **`effective_from` IS PER RULE AGAIN, and Alabama makes the point better than Arkansas did:** five different dates across 11 rows, from Rule 36's 1995-10-01 to Rule 6's **2026-04-09** -- the freshest rule text on the platform, amended four months ago. A single collection date would have been wrong for four of the five.
  **ONE ALABAMA ITEM IS DELIBERATELY UNRESOLVED.** Ala. Code 1-3-8(f)(1) lets an office STAY OPEN on a state holiday on 60 days' notice; if a court did and the engine rolls anyway, that is **LATE**. It is discretionary, per-office and published nowhere machine-readable, so it is **disclosed in the coverage summary rather than buried in the detail** -- and the gate had already said it should go to the bundled holiday question rather than be waved through. It is still there.
  **WISCONSIN: I ANSWERED THE GATE'S OPEN QUESTION AND IT REFUTES THE GATE'S OWN PREFERRED OPTION.** Sec. 6 listed as *not determined* whether Wisconsin's clerks publish closure schedules -- "which is what option 1 turns on". **They do:** `wicourts.gov/courts/circuit/docs/holidayschedule26.pdf` (200, 86,005 B, 3pp; the 2027 sheet 404s). **It is PER-COUNTY and the counties genuinely diverge** -- the sheet's own key is `X = Closed all day; PM = Closed in the afternoon; No Court = Courts are down but offices are OPEN`, and at least **12 of 72 counties** carry a non-closure marker on a listed holiday: **Dane is OPEN on Juneteenth, Barron is OPEN on Memorial Day**, six counties are PM-only on MLK Day. Since Wis. Stat. 801.15(1)(b) rolls on *"a day the clerk of courts office is closed"* and NOT on the 801.15(1)(a) list, **encoding the list as a rollover proxy would roll LATE in real counties.** Option 1 is now refuted by the court system's own publication rather than merely unproven.
  **PROPOSED, NOT CHOSEN -- option 4: seed the statewide INTERSECTION of actual closures** (only days every county is marked closed all day). Derived from a primary court-system publication, it can never roll LATE because any day some county is open is simply absent, and where it is wrong it is EARLY. Caps at 2026 like Arkansas and Utah. **This is a design decision with a legal consequence and it is Michael's, not the session's. Wisconsin stays unseeded pending that call**, and it is the same question Alabama's 1-3-8(f)(1) raises in a milder form -- a statutory holiday list is not the same fact as whether the courthouse was open.
  **844/844 deadline tests pass.** The nine-file coverage-set invariant has now caught two jurisdiction additions in a row, which is the argument for keeping it.

- sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_wisconsin.json + calendars + engine + docs (Hank) -- **WISCONSIN SEEDED ON OPTION 4 (12 rules, 16/16 live) -- AND I HAD TO CORRECT TWO CLAIMS I MADE THE DAY BEFORE.**
  **THE CORRECTION FIRST, because it is the more useful half.** On 2026-08-29 I wrote, in the gate doc, the index and two commit messages, that *"Dane is OPEN on Juneteenth"* and *"Barron is OPEN on Memorial Day"*. **Both were wrong.** They came from splitting the closure schedule's TEXT LAYER, which **drops blank cells and therefore shifts every marker left**. Re-read by CHARACTER POSITION (pdfplumber, x-coordinates of all 72 county rows) both counties' `Open` marker sits on **2026-07-03**, the observed Independence Day. The general conclusion was unaffected and is if anything stronger -- but the specifics were fabricated by a parsing artefact, and a misaligned marker here is a wrong legal deadline. Corrected in the gate doc and the index, on the record rather than quietly.
  **THE INTERSECTION, computed properly: 3 dates of 15.** New Year's Day, Memorial Day, Thanksgiving -- the only ones where all 72 counties are marked X. **The full spread is the argument for option 4:** 67 of 72 counties are NOT closed all day on Juneteenth, 69 of 72 on Columbus Day, 60 of 72 on Washington's Birthday, 55 on Veterans Day, 54 on MLK Day. Four dates were excluded on a SINGLE county each, which is the rule working rather than failing -- Labor Day (Brown prints `_`, an ambiguity, and ambiguity must not become a closure), Christmas Day (Kewaunee), Christmas Eve and day-after-Thanksgiving (Milwaukee), plus 07-03 (Barron, Dane, Chippewa).
  **THE UNDER-EXCLUSION IS ASSERTED, NOT JUST DISCLOSED.** Sec. 801.15(1)(b) keys the INTERMEDIATE-day exclusion to the statutory LIST while keying the ROLLOVER to CLOSURE, and one calendar cannot serve both. A 10-day period from 2026-06-11 returns **2026-06-25 live**, counting Juneteenth as an ordinary day; a list-based engine answers 06-26. Every error this calendar can make is in that direction, and the test pins the number rather than the adjective.
  **THE 5 P.M. BOUNDARY, both readings encoded and asserted live.** Sec. 801.15(5)(b) adds one day for fax/e-mail/e-filing "completed between 5 p.m. and midnight". **17:00 EXACTLY adds nothing** -- "between" does not settle the endpoint, and reading it inside would report LATE. **02:00 adds nothing either**, which a naive "after 5 p.m." implementation gets wrong. A missing `service_time` REFUSES rather than guessing between 0 and 1 (the Virginia precedent). All four cases verified against the deployed endpoint.
  **ONE QUESTION LEFT OPEN DELIBERATELY.** Sec. 801.15(5) adds days after service of "a notice or other paper" -- generic words, with **none of the Rule 4 / Rule 5 carve-out the federal rule, Arkansas and Alabama all carry**. Whether a summons and complaint is such a paper was not read, so the three original-process rows carry **NO extension**: applying it wrongly is three days LATE, withholding it is EARLY. Recorded on each row as the place to change if a Wisconsin reading settles it.
  **The 45-day INSURER-OR-TORT branch got its own row** with an unmissable trigger name, Missouri's treatment: it turns on what the claim is ABOUT, which the engine cannot infer, and it is the most commonly hit branch in practice since a large share of civil filings sound in tort. Using the default 20-day row for a tort claim reports 05-21 when the answer is really due 06-15.
  **"MAY" vs "SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED" for the third jurisdiction running** -- Secs. 804.08 and 804.09 give the defendant an ELECTION to take 45 days, only Sec. 804.11 imposes a FLOOR. Same split as Alabama and South Carolina.
  **`effective_from` is CONSERVATIVE and says so:** the statutes PDF prints a History line per section but no per-section effective DATE, so each row carries 1 January of the year FOLLOWING its last amending act -- a date that can only be LATER than the true one, so the engine refuses in the gap rather than computing against a rule not yet in force.
  **A REAL BUG THE SEED TEST CAUGHT:** the first generator built authority URLs from the SECTION number (`802.06.pdf`), and every one 404s -- the Legislature publishes per CHAPTER. The test asserts the URL SHAPE, not merely that a url field exists, which is the only reason it failed loudly.
  Live coverage is now **27 computing jurisdictions / 305 computing rules**, gate-verified (305/305 rules, 134/134 calendars, 0/0/0). **871/871 deadline tests pass.**

- sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_maryland.json + calendars + engine + docs (Hank) -- **MARYLAND SEEDED (11 rules, 14/14 live), BOTH DECISIONS IMPLEMENTED, AND THE ENGINE GAP CLOSED. Also: Wisconsin's open statutory question researched and closed.**
  **WISCONSIN FIRST.** The three original-process rows still take no service extension, but the note now says WHY on ch. 801's own structure rather than "not read": **Sec. 801.14 is titled "Service and filing of PLEADINGS AND OTHER PAPERS"** and its (2) defines mail service of a paper -- the exact act 801.15(5)(a) keys on; **the statute draws the line itself**, since 801.14(1) has to require certain pleadings to be served *"in the manner provided for service of summons in s. 801.11"*, and a statute that must cross-reference summons service by name to borrow it is not already describing it; and **a Wisconsin summons cannot be served by mail at all** -- 801.11(1) gives personal, substituted-at-abode, publication-WITH-mailing, and other-statute methods, with no plain mail method, so the trigger 801.15(5)(a) needs does not exist for original process. The one residual (801.11(1)(d)) argues for withholding, not granting. **The gate caught all three rows as STALE on a note-only change and went green after the reload, with the computed dates unchanged** -- a clean demonstration that it detects content drift rather than behaviour drift.
  **MARYLAND, DECISION ONE: refuse, don't guess.** `md_rule_1_203_c` returns the unextended date with a refusal naming BOTH readings when the period is <= 7 days. The reason it is a refusal rather than a choice: Rule 1-203(c) adds three days "to the prescribed period" and 1-203(a) excludes intermediate days when the period is "seven days or less", so on a mailed 4-7 day period **which reading is conservative depends on the length of the period** -- unlike every other ambiguity on this platform, there is no safe side. **NOT REACHABLE FROM THE SEED TODAY, and asserted so:** no seeded Maryland row is 7 days or shorter, so adding one later fails the suite and forces the question rather than silently arming a refusal nobody expected. Same shape as the quarantined LAST_OWNER guard.
  **DECISION TWO: caller supplies the computed date -- and it is FOUR rows, not the three the gate named.** Rule 2-311(b) has the identical chained shape hiding in the MOTIONS rule: *"within 15 days after being served with the motion, or within the time allowed for a party's original pleading pursuant to Rule 2-321(a), whichever is later."* Its second limb carries a **count of ZERO**, because the caller supplies the required date itself rather than a date to count from. All four rows carry Michael's reasoning verbatim: full chaining is disproportionate for one jurisdiction, refusing the limb throws away value on the highest-stakes rule in any gate, and requiring a date the party already knows is the middle ground.
  **THE ENGINE GAP IS CLOSED: `short_period_exclusion_directions`.** Optional and absent on every other standard, so all twelve that declare a threshold behave identically -- **871/871 unchanged when it landed**, which is the evidence it is inert rather than the claim. Maryland sets `['forward']` because Rule 1-203(b) counts backward periods *"including intervening Saturdays, Sundays, and holidays"* where (a) excludes them. Reusing forward logic backward reports **LATER**, which on a backward period means telling someone they may still serve after the last lawful day.
  **THE THRESHOLD IS 8, NOT THE 7 IN THE RULE.** "Seven days or less" is <= 7 which is < 8, and the field is a strict less-than -- second jurisdiction after Texas ("five days or less" -> 6) where the rule's number and the field's number differ. Asserted as arithmetic at exactly seven days, which is where writing 7 would break.
  **TWO BEHAVIOURS NO SEEDED ROW REACHES YET are asserted with a SYNTHETIC rule the test labels as such** rather than pretending it is coverage: the backward exclusion (5 days back from 2026-06-08 gives 06-03, where a wrongly-applied forward exclusion gives 06-01 -- two days later) and the mailed-short-period refusal.
  **THE WRONG-SOURCE TRAP is recorded in the coverage disclosure and verified live**, because it fails in BOTH directions at once: Gen. Provisions 1-302 rolls only on "a Sunday or legal holiday" with no Saturday roll (EARLY), and 1-111 adds Good Friday, Lincoln's Birthday, Maryland Day and Defenders' Day, which are not court holidays (LATE). Rule 1-203's own committee note settles it. The test asserts all four of those dates are ABSENT from the calendar.
  **Calendar INGESTED, 13 dates**, with two entries on no other calendar in this platform: **Election Day** (present only because 2026 is an even year, SPP 9-201(13)) and **American Indian Heritage Day** (the Friday after Thanksgiving, 9-201(11)). Independence Day is published as **Friday 3 July** -- the Judiciary applying the 9-204 Saturday shift itself, which is the published practice the gate relied on for the doctrinal gap between Rule 1-202(l) and 9-204.
  **A REAL PARSING BUG, caught because the crawler reported nothing rather than something wrong:** the Maryland rule index uses an EN DASH ("Rule 2-321" with U+2013), so a regex looking for `2-321` matched zero links and the first crawl printed `resolved: []`. Fixed and noted in the fetcher.
  Live coverage is now **28 computing jurisdictions / 316 computing rules**, gate-verified 316/316 and 135/135, 0/0/0. **904/904 deadline tests pass.**

- sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_kansas.json + calendars + engine + docs/sairnlaw-kansas-deadline-seed-gate.md (Hank) -- **KANSAS GATED AND SEEDED IN ONE PASS (10 rules, 14/14 live). A FRESH gate, because the queue was empty.** No gate-passed jurisdiction remained unseeded -- Alabama, Arkansas, Wisconsin and Maryland are all done, and every other gate doc is BLOCKED/GATED/FAILED. **Colorado is the largest that remains and I did NOT take it:** its block is a terms-acceptance decision the gate deliberately refused to make (the consolidated C.R.C.P. sits behind a LexisNexis "I Agree"), and that is Michael's call, not a session's. I re-probed the two leads anyway -- `courts.state.co.us/.../Updated_Full_set_of_CRCP_and_Water_Rules.pdf` returns HTML, not a PDF, exactly as the gate recorded.
  **KANSAS HAS THE BEST SOURCE POSITION SINCE WISCONSIN, and the reason is structural: it puts civil procedure in the STATUTE rather than in court rules.** K.S.A. ch. 60 art. 2 carries computation, answers, motions and all three discovery periods; the Office of Revisor of Statutes serves every section as free HTML on a plain request. No Westlaw hop, no Lexis hop, no click-through. **Access note worth keeping: `kscourts.gov/About-the-Courts/Programs/Rules` 403s, but `/Footer/Holiday-Schedule` and `/Rules-Orders/Rules/Terms-of-Court-Holidays` both 200** -- the 403 is path-specific, not host-wide.
  **EFFECTIVE DATES ARE PRINTED PER SECTION**, right after the last session law -- "L. 2020, ch. 4, § 3; March 19". No convention needed and none invented, unlike Wisconsin where the PDF gives a History line and no date and every row had to carry a conservative 1 January.
  **THE FINDING IS AN ABSENCE: Kansas has NO short-period exclusion.** 60-206(a)(1)(B) counts "every day, INCLUDING intermediate Saturdays, Sundays and legal holidays" -- it took the 2010 restyling that abolished the exclusion federally. **The tally across seeded states is now wide enough that copying is indefensible:** 7 for NJ/NC/WA/MA/MO/SC/OH/IN, **8** for Maryland, **11** for TN/AZ/WI/AL, **14** for Arkansas, **6** for Texas, and **none** for MN/UT/NV/KS. The test asserts the field is **undefined**, not merely small, and pins it as arithmetic on the seven-day backward row -- exactly where a Maryland-style value changes the answer.
  **TWO MORE CARRY-ACROSS TRAPS:** the service order is **FEDERAL after-expiry** (asserted against the date period-lengthening would have given on a base period landing Saturday before a holiday Monday), and there is **NO ELECTRONIC LIMB** -- 60-206(d) reaches mail and leaving-with-the-clerk only, so e-mail gets nothing, the opposite of Arkansas, Alabama and Massachusetts.
  **FIRST SEEDED JURISDICTION WHERE ALL THREE DISCOVERY PERIODS ARE ELECTIONS.** Every one says a defendant "MAY serve" within 45 days. Alabama and Wisconsin each make one of theirs mandatory, South Carolina makes admissions mandatory, Kansas makes none -- so all three are plain 30-day rows and **no Kansas row is a resolve_periods**, asserted as a count of zero.
  **AND IT IS NOT THE WISCONSIN PROBLEM -- this is the part worth carrying forward.** The Kansas schedule says a district court "may remain open on any of these designated holidays", language that looks identical to the Wisconsin hazard. The difference is **which fact the statute keys on**: Wisconsin rolls unless it is "a day the clerk of courts office is CLOSED", so a county staying open destroys the list as a proxy and forced the three-day intersection; **K.S.A. 60-206(a)(6) keys on the day being OBSERVED BY ORDER OF THE SUPREME COURT**, a statewide legal fact, so a district court opening its doors does not stop the day being a legal holiday. The list IS the test here. Recorded in the coverage entry and pinned by the test.
  Calendar: 12 dates, **two days for Thanksgiving** (26 and 27 Nov, both printed) and Independence Day published as Friday 3 July -- transcribed, not derived. **NOT ENCODED:** service by publication, where the answer is due "within the time fixed in the notice, which must not be less than 41 days" -- the 41 is a FLOOR on what the notice may say, not the period.
  Live coverage is now **29 computing jurisdictions / 326 computing rules**. **929/929 deadline tests pass.**

- CLAIMING: sairnlaw mississippi deadline seed gate -- claimed and released via `tools/sairn_claim.py` (hank, 2026-08-30). Result below.
- api/_lib/deadline-engine.js + api/_lib/deadline-mississippi.test.js + sql/sairnlaw_deadline_{seed,calendars}_mississippi.json + docs/sairnlaw-mississippi-deadline-seed-gate.md (Hank) -- **MISSISSIPPI GATED AND SEEDED IN ONE PASS (11 rules, loaded, 35/35 live). 30th computing jurisdiction; live coverage is now 30 jurisdictions / 337 rules, gate-verified 337/337 and 137/137, 0/0/0.**
  **FIRST, THE THING I WAS SENT FOR: COLORADO WAS ALREADY DONE.** The prior session's work landed as `42b3d05` before the context limit -- Michael authorised accepting the LexisNexis terms, the terms modal never re-appeared on that profile, and the real blocker turned out to be a CAPTCHA at `advance.lexis.com/RobotValidation?flow=PawFirstDocAccess`. Colorado stays BLOCKED and the terms question is moot. Also surfaced rather than swallowed: the SessionStart hook warned another session held the `hank` clone (PID 23260) -- **that PID is dead**, so it was a stale lock, not a live duplicate.
  **MISSISSIPPI IS THE MIRROR IMAGE OF COLORADO, which is why it was worth taking next.** The RULES are free, official and complete -- the whole MRCP as one 988,557-byte PDF, no gate, no sign-in. The CODE is the thing behind Lexis: courts.ms.gov's own "MS Code" link goes to `lexisnexis.com/hottopics/mscode/` which redirects to `advance.lexis.com`, the same container that stops Colorado. It did not block the gate, because the only thing the Code is needed for is the holiday definition, and the judicial branch restates it on its own State Library page and cites `Sec. 3-3-7(1)` in every closure notice.
  **THE SAME SOFT-404 TRAP AS COLORADO, on a different host.** `courts.ms.gov/research/rules/rules.php` carries TWO links captioned "Mississippi Rules of Civil Procedure". The first, dated 2026-06-18, is **inside an HTML comment** and the file behind it is gone -- requesting it returns **HTTP 200 with 17,429 bytes of HTML**, not a PDF, exactly the shape the Colorado gate recorded for `Updated_Full_set_of_CRCP_and_Water_Rules.pdf`. A scraper taking the first regex match reads nothing and `curl` calls it success. A test asserts no row cites it.
  **THE FINDING IS THE CALENDAR AND IT IS TWO DAYS LONG.** Three provisions have to agree, and for eight of the ten statutory holidays they do not. Rule 6(a) rolls off "a legal holiday, AS DEFINED BY STATUTE, or any other day when the courthouse or the clerk's office is IN FACT CLOSED". Sec. 3-3-7(1) supplies the definition but opens "EXCEPT AS OTHERWISE PROVIDED IN SUBSECTION (2)", and **Sec. 3-3-7(2) lets ANY county or municipality trade any ONE of them for Mardi Gras or any other day, excepting only the third Monday in January and 11 November.** Sec. 25-1-99 makes closure mandatory -- "the courthouse SHALL be closed on all state holidays as set forth in Section 3-3-7" -- but only for the days still on that list in that county, and its Friday-before-a-Saturday-holiday limb is "MAY", per county.
  **IT IS NOT THEORETICAL AND THAT IS WHAT SETTLED IT: JACKSON COUNTY HAS DONE IT.** Its published schedule lists ten holidays including **GOOD FRIDAY**, which is nowhere in Sec. 3-3-7, and **omits the last Monday in April** (Confederate Memorial Day), which Sec. 3-3-7 declares. Ten in, ten out -- a one-for-one substitution, on the record, in a real Gulf Coast county. A statewide calendar carrying 2026-04-27 would roll a Jackson County deadline off an OPEN courthouse and report LATER than Rule 6(a) allows.
  **SO THE CALENDAR IS THE STATUTORY INTERSECTION: 2026-01-19 and 2026-11-11, and nothing else.** Seven dates a Sec. 3-3-7 calendar would have carried are asserted LIVE not to roll: 02-16, 04-27, 05-25, 07-03, 09-07, 11-26, 12-25. **This is the Wisconsin design, not the Kansas one** -- Kansas keys its rollover on a day observed BY ORDER OF THE SUPREME COURT, a statewide legal fact, so its published list IS the test; Mississippi keys on a definition each county may lawfully alter. One point in Mississippi's favour: Wisconsin's three-day intersection came from reading a 72-county closure matrix and can go stale when a county changes practice; Mississippi's two days are written into **the statute's own exception clause**.
  **I WAS WRONG ONCE AND THE CORRECTION IS THE MOST USEFUL THING HERE. The first draft seeded Rule 56(c) backward** -- "the motion shall be served at least ten days before the time fixed for the hearing" -- reasoning that ten clears the seven-day exclusion threshold so the exclusion never fires. **That is true of the exclusion and irrelevant to the ROLLOVER.** Under-inclusion is EARLY only while the count runs forward; counting backward it inverts in BOTH mechanisms, because a last day that should have rolled BACK off an omitted holiday does not roll at all. A hearing on Thursday 2026-05-07 counts back to Monday 27 April, Confederate Memorial Day; in a county that still observes it the true last day is Friday 24 April, three days earlier. **So NO Mississippi row is seeded backward at all** -- Rule 6(d)'s five-day motion notice and one-day opposing affidavit go too -- and the test asserts the constraint over the seed file rather than leaving it to a reviewer. Rule 56(c) was replaced by Rules 59(b) and 59(e), both forward, both ten days from entry of judgment, and both on Rule 6(b)'s **non-enlargeable list**.
  **Rule 6(e) is PERIOD-LENGTHENING and MAIL ONLY, and the mail-only part is the finding**, because Mississippi has permitted electronic service since 1989: Rule 5(b)(1) names electronic means, leaving with the clerk and transmitting to the clerk electronically, and Rule 5(b)(2) routes service through MEC. Rule 6(e) was never widened. Ninth distinct answer on e-service across the gated states, and **stricter than Kansas**, which at least extends for leaving with the clerk. **The Rule 4 carve-out is EXPRESS** ("This subdivision does not apply to responses to service of summons under Rule 4") rather than inferred from the Rule 4 / Rule 5 split, and is proved live in both directions -- a mailed summons adds nothing to the answer, a mailed cross-claim takes the three days.
  **TWO ELECTIONS AND ONE FLOOR INSIDE ONE RULE SET.** Rules 33(b)(3) and 34(b)(ii)(A) each say a defendant "MAY serve" within 45 days; Rule 36(a) says a defendant "SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED to serve ... before the expiration of" 45 days. Only admissions is a resolve_periods. Arkansas made all three floors, Kansas all three elections; Mississippi joins South Carolina in making admissions the mandatory one -- and it is the one where silence ADMITS. Rule 36's floor also runs from "service of the SUMMONS" where 33 and 34 say "the summons and complaint"; the limb is named for the rule's own words.
  **A DEFECT WAS FOUND BY THE LOAD-STATE GATE AND IS REPORTED RATHER THAN HIDDEN.** The first load stored all 11 rows with a server-stamped `authority.retrieved_at`, because the seed rows omitted the field and `add_rule` defaults it; `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py` then reported **all 11 as STALE** against the repo. The computed dates were correct throughout -- the drift was provenance, not arithmetic -- but a licence that does not match its own seed file is precisely the state that gate exists to catch, and it caught it within minutes of the load. `retrieved_at: "2026-08-30"` added to every row, reloaded, clean. **Worth carrying forward: a new seed's rules must carry `retrieved_at` explicitly, or the gate red-flags the whole jurisdiction on its first load.**
  **NOT SEEDED, each for a stated reason:** Rule 50(b) (ten days, but from two alternative triggers depending on whether a verdict was returned -- the engine cannot tell which applies from one date); Rule 34(b)(ii)(B)'s production itself and Rule 56(c)'s opposing affidavits (both set by the parties' own papers, not by the rule); Rule 12(a)'s "such time as is directed pursuant to Rule 4", its once-only ten-day stipulated extension and the court-ordered reply's "unless the order otherwise directs" (all caller-supplied facts); M.R.A.P. 4's appeal clock, which a timely Rule 59 motion tolls but which is an appellate rule out of this seed's domain -- the 59(e) row says so in its own note so nobody infers an appeal date from it.
  Live coverage is now **30 computing jurisdictions / 337 computing rules**. **969/969 deadline tests pass.** 2026-08-30

  **THE HEADLINE IS A DELETION AND IT RUNS LATE.** Supreme Court Order No. S-1-RCR-2023-00046, approved 1 November 2024 and effective for all cases pending or filed on or after **31 December 2024, STRUCK "electronic transmission," from Rule 1-006(C) NMRA.** Mail, facsimile and deposit at a court-facility attorney location still take three days; e-service and e-filing take NOTHING. **Every secondary source I hit still quotes the pre-2024 list**, including the first page of search results. Granting the three days is three days LATE on every e-served deadline in the state.
  **I READ BOTH TEXTS ON PURPOSE AND NEITHER WOULD HAVE DONE ALONE.** New Mexico's approved-amendment PDFs are REDLINES -- bracketed text is deleted. The 1 Nov 2024 PDF shows `[electronic transmission,]` struck and `[Columbus Day]Indigenous Peoples Day` swapped, and the second is self-verifying, which is what establishes the convention for the first. But a redline is not the operative text, so the clean compilation was read too and confirms 1-006(C) now says "by mail, facsimile, or by deposit". **The redline shows that something was REMOVED, which the clean text can never show; the clean text shows the removal is IN FORCE, which the redline can never show.**
  **THE SOURCE IS FREE AND OFFICIAL AND NOT WHERE A GATE WOULD LOOK.** `supremecourt.nmcourts.gov` publishes one PDF per rule but ONLY for rules recently amended, so 1-012, 1-036 and 1-056 are not there at all -- a gate stopping there concludes three of six rules do not exist. The complete NMRA is on NMOneSource.com (the Compilation Commission, "Official Legal Publisher of State of New Mexico"), free with optional login, and a **Lexum single-page app whose nav endpoints return 404 JSON to curl**. Every guessed URL 404'd. **Driving it once in a browser produced the one URL that matters** -- `nmonesource.com/nmos/nmra/en/5687/1/document.do`, Rule Set 1 as a plain 5,668,940-byte / 1,072-page PDF on an ordinary curl, no gate, no sign-in. The JavaScript was an obstacle, not a wall. Contrast Colorado, where the wall is real.
  **ELEVEN, NOT THE TEN THE RULE PRINTS.** 1-006(A)(2) excludes intermediate weekends and holidays at "ten (10) days or less" and the field is a strict less-than. Third jurisdiction after Texas (6) and Maryland (8) where the rule's number and the field's number differ, and the one where it bites hardest: both Rule 1-012(A) responsive-pleading rows are EXACTLY ten days.
  **PRESIDENTS' DAY IS OBSERVED IN NOVEMBER.** Rule 1-006(A)(7)(a) says it in a parenthetical -- "Presidents' Day (traditionally observed on the day after Thanksgiving)" -- and the Chief Justice's 2026 memo puts it on **Friday 27 November 2026**. No other calendar in this platform moves a holiday to the far side of the year. **A generated third-Monday-in-February date is wrong twice at once**: it adds 16 February, which the judiciary does not observe, and drops 27 November, which it does. Both halves are asserted live.
  **THE CALENDAR IS THE STATUTORY TEST, NOT A PROXY -- the Kansas shape.** 1-006(A)(7) keys on what "the judiciary" observes, New Mexico's district courts are state administered, and the Chief Justice issues one branch-wide schedule. That is the opposite of Wisconsin and Mississippi, whose rollovers key on something a county can vary and whose calendars are therefore defensive intersections. Eleven dates, transcribed from the memo of Chief Justice David K. Thomson dated 19 Nov 2025, every printed weekday re-checked.
  **A SHORT BACKWARD ROW IS SAFE HERE, AND THAT IS THE DIRECT CONTRAST WITH MISSISSIPPI FROM EARLIER TODAY.** 1-006(A)(6): "the 'next day' is determined by continuing to count FORWARD when the period is measured after an event and BACKWARD when measured before an event." Only Florida also says this. Mississippi is silent on direction AND under-inclusive on its calendar, so every backward row there had to be dropped; New Mexico states the direction and its calendar is complete, so **Rule 1-055(B)'s three-day default-judgment notice -- short, backward, the exact shape Mississippi refused -- is seeded and live-verified** (hearing 16 Nov 2026 -> 2026-11-10, counting back over Veterans Day).
  **THE ORDER IS FEDERAL AFTER-EXPIRY AND NEW MEXICO IS THE ONLY SEEDED STATE WHOSE TEXT SPELLS OUT THE WHOLE SEQUENCE:** three days "added after the period would otherwise expire", intermediate weekends and holidays "INCLUDED in counting these added three (3) days", then a roll if the third day lands badly. That settles a question every other state leaves open -- the added days count straight through even when the BASE period excluded them -- and one live date proves all of it: 2026-12-02 + 3 = Saturday 5 Dec -> Monday **2026-12-07**.
  **THE ASYMMETRY IS THE RULE'S, NOT A TYPO TO TIDY.** Rule 1-005(C)(1)(b) makes "sending a copy by facsimile or electronic transmission" ONE sub-limb of what delivering a copy means -- siblings in the service rule -- and 1-006(C) now reaches one and not the other.
  **NO CARVE-OUT FOR SERVICE OF PROCESS, SO ONE WAS READ RATHER THAN QUOTED -- same position as Wisconsin, resolved the same way.** 1-005(A) governs "every pleading SUBSEQUENT TO THE ORIGINAL COMPLAINT", original process is Rule 1-004, and 1-006(C) defines its own third method by cross-reference INTO 1-005(C)(1)(e). The answer row carries no extension field; withholding reports EARLY, granting on a mailed summons would report LATE. Proved live both ways: mailed summons 2026-12-16 unchanged, mailed cross-claim 2026-12-21.
  **A ROW WAS DROPPED FOR WANT OF A DATE, AND THAT IS WORTH RECORDING.** Rule 1-027(A)(2)'s twenty-day notice was drafted into the seed and removed: **no amendment bracket and no amendment year mentioned anywhere in the rule**, so there was no defensible `effective_from` and no honest convention to reach for. Rule 1-068(A)'s ten-day acceptance window, which carries `[As amended, effective August 1, 2003.]`, took its place. One date IS a convention and is labelled as one -- Rule 1-036 has no bracket either, but its annotations refer three times to a "1979 amendment", so it takes 1980-01-01, Wisconsin's rule.
  **2027 IS REFUSED, AND THE TEMPTATION WAS SPECIFIC.** The 2026 memo announces exactly one 2027 date, New Year's Day on Friday 1 Jan 2027. Opening a 2027 calendar on that single entry would let 2027 deadlines compute against a year missing ten of its eleven days -- an answer dressed as coverage. A test asserts the calendar holds `['2026']` and nothing else.
  **THIRTY, NOT TEN, ON THE POST-JUDGMENT MOTIONS, and the committee commentary says the old number out loud:** "With the exception of Rule 1-060 NMRA, the time limit HAD BEEN ten (10) days." The 2013 amendment moved them all to thirty; a ten-day figure from an older source reports TWENTY DAYS EARLY, and 1-006(B)(2) forbids the court to enlarge any of them.
  **TWO ELECTIONS AND ONE FLOOR AGAIN** -- 1-033 and 1-034 say a defendant "MAY serve" within 45 days, 1-036 says "shall not be required ... before the expiration of". New Mexico joins Mississippi and South Carolina in making admissions the mandatory one, and it is self-executing.
  **NOT SEEDED, each for a stated reason:** Rule 1-027(A)(2) (no effective date); Rule 1-068(A)'s "at any time MORE THAN ten (10) days before the trial begins" (a strict inequality, not a period -- converting it to a backward eleven would be a reading); 1-068's 120-day claimant wait (runs from a filing date the engine is not given); 1-056(D)(1)'s "reasonable time prior to trial" (a standard); 1-034(B)'s production itself (set by the parties' papers); 1-006(A)(3)'s HOURS arithmetic and (A)(5)'s split cut-off, midnight for e-filing and closing time otherwise, which this engine cannot express because it returns a date rather than a moment; and Rule 12-201's appeal clock, which the 1-059 rows toll but which is appellate.
  Live coverage is now **31 computing jurisdictions / 352 computing rules**. **1010/1010 deadline tests pass.** 2026-08-30
