  **IDAHO PUBLISHES THREE HOLIDAY LISTS AND THEY DISAGREE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS. That is the finding.** (1) **Idaho Code Sec. 73-108** is what I.R.C.P. 2.2's undefined "legal holiday" refers to -- no Idaho court rule defines it, checked in both the civil and appellate rules, and Sec. 67-5302(15)(a) confirms the chain in terms: "Holidays are enumerated in section 73-108, Idaho Code." Eleven entries, **NO JUNETEENTH**, and both observance shifts MANDATORY. (2) The **Secretary of State's** published State Holidays page DOES carry Juneteenth on Fri 19 June 2026, and does NOT apply the Saturday shift -- it prints Independence Day as Saturday 4 July with nothing on the Friday. (3) **Actual court closures** are a third set again: the Idaho Supreme Court published a 2026 release headed "Idaho Courts to be Open July 2 & 6", keeping courts open as essential services on days other state government closes, while at least one county trial court closes for Juneteenth. **A calendar copied from the executive branch is wrong twice, in opposite directions.**
  **THE CALENDAR IS THE STATUTE AND IS DELIBERATELY UNDER-INCLUSIVE.** Juneteenth's only route in is Sec. 73-108's open limb for days "appointed by the President ... or by the governor", a proclamation this engine cannot read. Omitting reports EARLY, adding would report LATE. **Friday 3 July 2026 IS carried, DERIVED from the mandatory Saturday shift** -- live-verified: a 21-day period from 12 June lands there and rolls through the weekend to Mon 6 July, where an SOS-derived calendar answers 3 July. Both halves probed live: Juneteenth does NOT roll, Columbus Day DOES (Idaho enumerates it; Oregon does not), the day after Thanksgiving does NOT.
  **THE RULE NUMBER IS A TRAP IN ITSELF.** Idaho restyled in 2016 and moved computation of time **from Rule 6 to RULE 2.2**. "Idaho Rule 6" has pointed at nothing since 1 July 2016. Only seeded state that moved it.
  **NO SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION AT ALL** -- 2.2(a)(1)(B) counts "every day, INCLUDING intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays". Joins MN/UT/NV/KS. **It matters more here than the tally suggests:** four seeded rows are FOURTEEN days, which Alabama's and Wisconsin's 11 would still count straight through (right answer, wrong reason) but **Arkansas's 14 would exclude**. The test asserts the field is undefined rather than small.
  **FOUR WORDS SEPARATE IDAHO FROM MISSISSIPPI AND THEY DECIDE A WHOLE ROW.** Both states run the post-Rule-12-motion period from "notice of the court's action". Miss. R. Civ. P. 6(e) extends for "the service of a NOTICE or other paper"; I.R.C.P. 2.2(c) extends only "after service", with no notice limb. **Proved live across both jurisdictions on the same trigger name:** `notice_of_court_action_denying_or_postponing_motion` returns 2026-11-30 for Idaho with and without mail, and 2026-11-26 / 2026-11-30 for Mississippi. Idaho's own sibling row, which runs from SERVICE of a more definite statement, DOES take the three days. Same rule, same paragraph, same fourteen days, opposite answers, decided by which verb the subparagraph uses.
  **RULE 2.2(c) IS MAIL ONLY AND THAT IS NOT A LEFTOVER.** Mississippi's mail-only rule dates from 1982 and was never widened. **Idaho wrote this sentence from scratch in 2016**, when electronic service was already routine, and still reached only mail. Live: electronic and facsimile both get nothing, while New Mexico's facsimile limb still pays out on its own facts -- so the narrower list did not leak.
  **NO DEFENDANT FLOOR ANYWHERE -- A FIRST.** Rules 33(b)(2), 34(b)(2)(A) and 36(a)(4) are each a flat 30 days and **the string "45 days" does not appear anywhere in the I.R.C.P.** Not a floor, not an election. Idaho is the **first seeded state outside the federal seed itself where no discovery row is a resolve_periods**, and the reason is an absence rather than a reading. Arkansas made all three floors, Kansas all three elections, MS and NM two elections and one floor, NC 45/45/60, MA none/45/45. **The stakes sit in Rule 36, which is self-executing:** a floor read across from any neighbour would tell an Idaho defendant the matter is not yet admitted when it already is. Live-verified against NM on comparable facts -- 2026-11-02 vs 2026-11-04.
  **NO BACKWARD ROW IS SEEDED, AND IDAHO HAS GOOD ONES.** Rule 55(a)(1) requires three days' notice before entry of DEFAULT -- unusual, most states require it only before default JUDGMENT -- Rule 55(b)(2) three days before the judgment hearing, Rule 56(b)(2) 28/14/7 days before a hearing, Rule 56(b)(1) the motion 90 days before trial. All omitted. 2.2(a)(1)(C) rolls to "the NEXT day" and says nothing about a period measured before an event -- the Mississippi shape, not New Mexico's -- and with one annually-recurring date genuinely contested, under-inclusion is EARLY forward and LATE backward. **Seeding an Idaho backward row needs the Juneteenth question answered, not a longer period.**
  **THE SOURCE IS THE THIRD JS-FRONTED PUBLISHER IN THREE STATES AND THE THIRD DIFFERENT ANSWER.** isc.idaho.gov is a Nuxt app; /rules/ and /ircp/ 404 and the homepage is nothing but /_nuxt/*.js. But **/rules-procedure/ircp returns the ENTIRE I.R.C.P. server-rendered -- 104 rules, 1,445,212 bytes -- on a plain curl**, no gate. Colorado is a WALL (CAPTCHA behind terms). New Mexico was an OBSTACLE (one browser pass produced a plain PDF URL). Idaho was not even that; only my guessed URLs were wrong. Completeness checked, not assumed: 104 rule headings extracted and every needed rule found.

Read literally it names a day rather than a period. The seed records the quote exactly as published and says so in the row's note, rather than silently correcting the official text.
  **RULE 2.2(b) IS UNUSUALLY GENEROUS AND THEN UNUSUALLY STRICT:** (b)(2) lets the PARTIES extend time by written stipulation filed before or after expiry -- broader than most states -- and (b)(3) then freezes Rules 50(b), 52(b), 59(b), (d), (e) and 60(b) absolutely. Live: 59(b) and 59(e) take nothing on mail, 59(c) takes three days.

- api/_lib/deadline-engine.js + api/_lib/deadline-nebraska.test.js + sql/sairnlaw_deadline_{seed,calendars}_nebraska.json + docs/sairnlaw-nebraska-deadline-seed-gate.md (Hank) -- **NEBRASKA GATED AND SEEDED IN ONE PASS (10 rules, loaded, 30/30 live). 33rd computing jurisdiction; live coverage is now 33 jurisdictions / 373 rules, gate-verified 373/373 and 140/140, 0/0/0.**
  **A COURT NAMED THIS ENGINE'S EXACT AMBIGUITY AND AMENDED ITS RULE TO SETTLE IT.** Comment [2] to Neb. Ct. R. Pldg. Sec. 6-1106: *"The original version of the rule provided that 3 days were added to the applicable time period when a document was served by mail. IT WAS UNCLEAR WHETHER THE 3 DAYS WERE ADDED TO THE TIME PERIOD ITSELF OR AT THE END OF THE TIME PERIOD as computed by Sec. 25-2221. In 2024, the provision ... was reworded to clarify that the 3 days are added after the period would otherwise expire."* That is exactly `add_to_period_then_roll` vs `roll_then_add_then_roll` -- identified as genuinely ambiguous by the body that wrote the rule, and fixed by amendment. **Every other jurisdiction here had to be READ for this distinction; Nebraska is external confirmation that the care spent on it was warranted.** It also means any Nebraska mailed deadline computed against the pre-2025 wording may be a day out.
  **AND COMMENT [3] WORKS AN EXAMPLE, WHICH THE ENGINE IS ASSERTED AGAINST DIRECTLY** -- 30 days ending on a Saturday expires Monday, plus three gives THURSDAY. Interrogatories served 2026-02-19 return **2026-03-23 unmailed and 2026-03-26 mailed**, live. A third assertion pins that period-lengthening's Tuesday 24 March is NOT what comes back. First primary-source worked example on the platform.
  **THE PRIOR RULE VERSIONS ARE STILL PUBLISHED, AND THIS IS A MORE DANGEROUS TRAP THAN MISSISSIPPI'S.** nebraskajudicial.gov lists 'Article 11 ... (Effective January 1, 2025)' beside 'Prior Version of Article 11 ... (Effective before January 1, 2025)', and the same for Article 3. **Both are real, complete, correctly-labelled documents.** Mississippi's superseded link was a dead soft-404 -- a scraper got nothing and a reader noticed. Here a scraper gets a complete and plausible rule set that simply is not the law. **THE 2025 AMENDMENTS MOVED REAL NUMBERS:** post-motion responsive pleading 20 -> 21, more definite statement 20 -> 21, reply after an order to reply **15 -> 21**. A seed off the prior page is one day early on two rows and six days early on a third. Every row carries the current number with effective_from 2025-01-01, and a pre-2025 trigger REFUSES -- asserted locally and live.
  **THE COMPUTATION RULE IS A STATUTE AND THE COURT RULE SAYS SO IN ITS FIRST LINE:** Sec. 6-1106(a), "Neb. Rev. Stat. Sec. 25-2221 governs the computation of time periods." No chain to trace, the exact opposite of Idaho hours earlier. **AND Sec. 25-2221 CARRIES ITS OWN HOLIDAY LIST IN THE SAME SENTENCE AS ITS ROLLOVER TEST** -- it rolls off "a day during which the offices of courts of record may be legally closed AS PROVIDED IN THIS SECTION" and then enumerates them. No other seeded jurisdiction manages that; Kansas points at a supreme court order, New Mexico at judiciary observance, Idaho at a statute in another title, Mississippi at a statute counties can amend. **Strongest source position gated so far.**
  **NO SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION** -- sixth with none, after MN/UT/NV/KS/ID. **NO CITATION SUFFIXES:** Sec. 25-2221 is a single unsubdivided section, so every suffix on the standard is empty rather than guessed, and a test asserts that.
  **ARBOR DAY EXISTS ON NO OTHER CALENDAR IN THIS PLATFORM.** Last Friday in April -- a FLOATING date, 24 April in 2026 -- and Nebraska is the only state to make the holiday it invented a nonjudicial day. **THE CONTRAST WITH ITS NEIGHBOUR IS THREE DAYS WIDE:** Idaho Code Sec. 73-108 has no Juneteenth and no day after Thanksgiving; Nebraska has both, enumerated, plus Arbor Day. **Both asserted LIVE in both directions against both jurisdictions on the same platform** -- Juneteenth rolls in ne (2026-06-22) and does not in id (2026-06-19); the day after Thanksgiving rolls in ne (2026-11-30) and does not in id (2026-11-27). Two adjacent statutory lists, three days apart, seeded within hours of each other.
  **THE FEDERAL-OVERRIDE CLAUSE IS UNIQUE ON THE PLATFORM AND IS DORMANT IN 2026.** Sec. 25-2221: "If the date designated by the state for observance of any legal holiday pursuant to this section, EXCEPT VETERANS DAY, is different from the date of observance ... pursuant to a FEDERAL holiday schedule, the FEDERAL holiday schedule shall be observed." No other state subordinates its own dates to the federal calendar. Every 2026 date was checked against the federal schedule and none diverges, so it changes nothing this year -- but it is live law in a year where they split, it is NOT modelled, and Veterans Day is expressly carved out.
  **MAIL ONLY, AND THE DRAFTING IS THE SHARPEST ON THE PLATFORM.** Sec. 6-1106(c) does not say "by mail" -- it cross-references ONE lettered subparagraph, Sec. 6-1105(b)(3)(C), out of six siblings listed beside it: handing, leaving at an office or residence, MAILING, email, a designated delivery service, any other consented means -- with electronic service through the court-authorized provider a separate limb again. Five named alternatives get nothing. Asserted live on email, designated delivery service and e-filing, all returning the unextended date.
  **THE NOTICE/SERVICE SPLIT IS THE IDAHO SHAPE, NOT THE MISSISSIPPI ONE.** Sec. 6-1106(c) reaches a party acting "after BEING SERVED", with no notice limb; Miss. R. Civ. P. 6(e) reaches "the service of a NOTICE or other paper". So Nebraska's post-motion row takes nothing and its more-definite-statement sibling takes three. **Three states now run the same trigger words to two different answers, decided by the TIME rule rather than the pleading rule.**
  **ALL THREE DISCOVERY RULES ARE later_of AND SAY SO THEMSELVES** -- "45 days after being served with the summons or 30 days after being served with the [request], WHICHEVER IS LONGER". The rule does its own resolving, so no reading was needed -- which matters because 6-333 also says "MAY", the word that marks an ELECTION in Kansas and in Mississippi's Rules 33/34. Here "may" and "whichever is longer" sit in one sentence and the comparative governs. **Idaho has none of it: "45 days" does not appear in the I.R.C.P. at all.** Live on identical facts: ne 2026-11-04, id 2026-11-02.
  **A DEAD SECTION, RECORDED BECAUSE A PRE-2000 SOURCE SENDS YOU TO IT:** Neb. Rev. Stat. **Sec. 25-1143** (old new-trial section) was REPEALED by Laws 2000, LB 921, Sec. 38; the Legislature's page returns nothing but "Repealed." The motion is now Sec. 25-1144.01.
  **ONE EFFECTIVE DATE IS A CONVENTION AND IS LABELLED AS ONE.** Both post-judgment statutes print a source history (Laws 2000 LB 921; Laws 2004 LB 1207) and NO effective date, so they take 2005-01-01 -- the 1 January following the last amending act, Wisconsin's rule. **My first draft invented 2004-07-16 and it was caught and replaced before commit.**
  **NO BACKWARD ROW IS SEEDED.** Sec. 25-2221 rolls to "the NEXT day on which the office will be open" and says nothing about a period measured before an event -- the Mississippi and Idaho shape. The calendar is materially more complete than Idaho's so the case is weaker, but the direction question has not been read out of any Nebraska text and is not being guessed.
  Live coverage is now **33 computing jurisdictions / 373 computing rules**. **1090/1090 deadline tests pass.** 2026-08-30

- FLORIDA COVERAGE FIX (Hank, 2026-08-31) -- api/_lib/deadline-engine.js + api/_lib/deadline-florida-coverage.test.js. **Michael's decision: fix it, but as its own explicitly-scoped task, not folded into the citation audit that found it.** Done that way; disclosure only, no seed row, no calendar and no computation standard changed, and a control assertion pins the pre-change arithmetic (service Tue 1 Sep 2026 still returns 2026-09-21, the shifted start making Wednesday day one).
  **AND MY EARLIER FRAMING WAS LOOSE -- THE GAP WAS WHERE THE DISCLOSURE LIVED, NOT WHETHER IT EXISTED.** Florida's chief-justice limb has been documented since the jurisdiction was seeded, in the calendar's `authority.note`, naming 2.514(a)(6)(B), (a)(1)(C) and (a)(3)(C) and calling it "Florida's HURRICANE MECHANISM". But **an authority note is stored on the row and is never returned to a caller.** Thirteen other jurisdictions ride their caveat on the compute response through JURISDICTION_COVERAGE; Florida did not, so a Florida answer arrived with no warning attached.
  **READ VERBATIM FROM THE CURRENT RULE rather than from the existing note** -- the 1 July 2026 edition, 1,165,681 bytes, 259 pages, floridabar.org. It reaches an emergency order in THREE subdivisions: (a)(1)(C) rolls the last day if it "falls within a time extended by order of the chief justice"; (a)(3)(C) says the same for hours; (a)(6)(B) defines "legal holiday" to include "any day observed as a holiday by the CLERK'S OFFICE or as designated by the CHIEF JUSTICE OR CHIEF JUDGE". **Four unknowable sources across three subdivisions**, and the chief-JUDGE limb is per-circuit -- the only place Florida resembles Wisconsin. Naming only the famous one would have understated it.
  **THE DIRECTION IS EARLY AND THE ENTRY STATES THE CONDITION RATHER THAN ASSERTING IT FLATLY.** It holds only because every seeded Florida row is forward and no shorter than 20 days -- none backward, none in hours, none under 2.514(a)(2)'s 7-day threshold. **2.514(a)(5) DOES define "next day" as counting backward for a period measured before an event**, so a backward Florida row would invert it. The entry says "THIS REASONING STOPS BEING TRUE" in those words and three assertions re-check the seed's shape, so the claim fails loudly rather than going quietly false. Live-verified: the caveat now comes back on the wire, Nebraska's is unchanged, and North Carolina still returns `coverage: null` -- so nothing leaked onto jurisdictions that declare none.

  **TWO DAYS FOR MAILED SERVICE, NOT THREE, AND HAWAIʻI IS THE ONLY ONE.** Haw. R. Civ. P. 6(e): "2 days shall be added to the prescribed period." Every FRCP-family state adds three; SC and NJ add five; CA and NY use per-method tables. **An `add: 3` copied from any neighbour over-counts by a day on every mailed Hawaiʻi deadline -- the direction that reports LATE.** Asserted twice: as a field (no row carries 3) and **as a DATE** -- base Friday 20 March 2026 plus two is Sunday, rolled to Monday the 23rd, where three would have given Tuesday the 24th. Live-verified alongside Idaho and Nebraska still adding three on their own facts in the same run.
  **FOUR HOLIDAYS THAT EXIST ON NO OTHER CALENDAR IN THIS PLATFORM:** Prince Jonah Kuhio Kalanianaole Day (26 March), King Kamehameha I Day (11 June), Statehood Day (third Friday in August, a FLOATING date), and **GOOD FRIDAY -- the only computed-from-Easter date in the whole engine.** HRS Sec. 8-1 says "the Friday preceding Easter Sunday", so it is derived from the computus: Easter 2026 is 5 April, Good Friday 3 April. It is also the only religious observance any seeded state makes a legal holiday, and the statute's own case note records that it survived an Establishment Clause challenge, 932 F.2d 765. All four verified live to roll.
  **AND THREE ITS NEIGHBOURS HAVE THAT IT DOES NOT:** no Juneteenth, no Columbus/Indigenous Peoples' Day, no day after Thanksgiving -- **Nebraska enumerates all three.** A calendar copied from Nebraska would add three days Hawaiʻi lacks and miss four it has.
  **THE HOLIDAY REFERENT IS EXPRESS, WHICH IS EXACTLY WHAT IDAHO'S WAS NOT.** Rule 6(a) closes: "As used in these rules, 'holiday' shall mean any day designated as such pursuant to SECTION 8-1 of the Hawaiʻi Revised Statutes." One section, named by number. Idaho's rule said "legal holiday" and stopped, and three published lists disagreed. Note also that Rule 6(a) uses the bare word "holiday" throughout and never "legal holiday", which is why that closing sentence is load-bearing rather than decorative.
  **THE SEC. 8-2 WEEKEND SHIFT IS DELIBERATELY OMITTED, AND IT IS A READING.** HRS Sec. 8-2 observes a Saturday holiday on the preceding Friday. Rule 6(a) incorporates **section 8-1 by number**, and a Sec. 8-2 day is designated pursuant to Sec. 8-2. **Idaho and Nebraska both CARRY their shifts because there the clause sits in the SAME section the rule points at** -- Idaho Code Sec. 73-108 and Neb. Rev. Stat. Sec. 25-2221 each contain their own. Hawaiʻi's does not. Both readings are respectable so the direction decides: omitting is EARLY, carrying would be LATE. **In 2026 it affects exactly one date -- Friday 3 July** -- and the negative is probed live against Idaho and Nebraska both rolling their own 3 July to the 6th. A practitioner will treat that Friday as a court holiday; the coverage entry says so and names the date.
  **THE GENERAL ELECTION DAY IS ALSO OMITTED:** Sec. 8-1 designates "all election days, except primary and special election days, IN THE COUNTY WHEREIN THE ELECTION IS HELD" -- county-scoped by its own words and needing a second statute to date. Neither was resolved on a primary source, so it is omitted rather than computed rather than guessed at.
  **THE NOTICE LIMB IS NOW A THIRD ANSWER ACROSS FOUR STATES.** Rule 6(e) reaches "the service of a NOTICE or other paper", matching Mississippi; I.R.C.P. 2.2(c) and Neb. Ct. R. Pldg. Sec. 6-1106(c) reach only "after service". So on the identical trigger "notice of the court's action": Mississippi +3, **Hawaiʻi +2**, Idaho nothing, Nebraska nothing. **Decided every time by the TIME rule, never the pleading rule.** All four asserted live in one run.
  **NO BACKWARD ROW IS SEEDED, AND HAWAIʻI HAS THE LONGEST MOTION NOTICE ON THE PLATFORM** -- Rule 6(d) requires a motion and notice of hearing served NOT LESS THAN 18 DAYS before the hearing, and opposing affidavits not less than 8. **Both clear the seven-day threshold, so the exclusion is not the obstacle here -- the missing direction rule is, on its own.**
  **RULE 6(b)'s NON-EXTENDABLE LIST REACHES INTO ANOTHER RULE SET,** which no other seeded jurisdiction's Rule 6 does: it freezes Rules 50(b), 52(b), 59(b),(d),(e), 60(b) "and Rule 4(a) of the Hawaiʻi Rules of Appellate Procedure".
  **TWO ELECTIONS AND ONE FLOOR** (33 and 34 say "may", 36 says "shall not be required ... before the expiration of"), joining MS/NM/SC. **And contrast Nebraska the day before:** its Sec. 6-333(b)(2) says "may" AND "whichever is longer" in one sentence, so the comparative governs and the same word produces a floor. The word alone does not settle it; the sentence does.
  **THE UPLOAD PATH IS MISLEADING AND IS RECORDED AS SUCH:** the rule set lives under /wp-content/uploads/**2024/09**/ yet its own text records amendments effective **1 July 2026**. The file is maintained in place, so **a path is not a publication date** -- a gate inferring staleness from the URL would reject a current document.
  **RECORDED BECAUSE IT IS RECENT, NOT BECAUSE ANYTHING IS KNOWN TO HAVE MOVED:** Rule 6 was amended effective 1 Jan 2026, corrected 19 Dec 2025 and amended again effective 1 July 2026; Rule 36 amended effective 1 Jan 2026 (which is that row's effective_from). **WHAT those amendments changed was NOT determined** -- amendment history, no redline -- and the coverage entry says so rather than implying it was checked.
  Live coverage is now **34 computing jurisdictions / 384 computing rules**. **1150/1150 deadline tests pass.** 2026-08-31

- api/_lib/deadline-engine.js + api/_lib/deadline-newhampshire.test.js + sql/sairnlaw_deadline_{seed,calendars}_newhampshire.json + docs/sairnlaw-newhampshire-deadline-seed-gate.md (Hank) -- **NEW HAMPSHIRE GATED AND SEEDED IN ONE PASS (13 rules, 50/50 local). NOT LOADED AND NOT LIVE-VERIFIED -- this clone has no SAIRNLAW_LICENSE_KEY and .env.local is empty. Every number below is LOCAL. The seed-gate push hook will correctly DENY a push until the load runs.** Local: 1200/1200 deadline assertions across the suite (it was 1150/1150 before), 0 failing files across all 52 api/_lib tests, node --check clean on the engine, the new test and all 15 edited tests.
  **THERE IS NO MAILED-SERVICE EXTENSION. NOT A SHORTER ONE -- NONE, AND NEW HAMPSHIRE IS THE FIRST.** Every seeded jurisdiction until now had one: the FRCP family adds three, SC and NJ add five, Hawaiʻi adds two, CA and NY use per-method tables. The phrases "shall be added", "additional days" and "prescribed period" do not occur anywhere in the Superior Court Civil Rules or in the Supplemental Rules for Electronic Filing; the ONLY days-added-shaped sentence in the whole rule set is Rule 26(b)'s backward deposition notice. **An `add: 3` copied from any neighbour reports LATE on every New Hampshire deadline -- the direction that loses a filing.** Asserted as a FIELD (no row carries one, no nh_* key in SERVICE_EXTENSION_STANDARDS) and as a DATE: all thirteen rows computed four ways -- mail, electronic, facsimile, other_consented_means -- must return the identical answer, because the field being absent is necessary and not sufficient. Idaho, Nebraska and Hawaiʻi still return 3, 3 and 2 on their own rows in the same run.
  **AND THE REASON IS STRUCTURAL, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** New Hampshire runs its periods from FILING and from THE DATE ON THE CLERK'S NOTICE where the FRCP family runs them from SERVICE. Rule 13(a): 10 days "after FILING thereof". Rule 12(e): 10 days "of the DATE ON THE WRITTEN NOTICE ..., WHICH SHALL BE MAILED OR ELECTRONICALLY DELIVERED BY THE CLERK ON THE DATE OF THE NOTICE." Rule 43: 10 days "from the date on the court's written notice ..., WHICH SHALL BE MAILED BY THE COURT ON THE DATE OF THE NOTICE." **Each sentence names the delivery method and then declines to let it matter.** A rule that starts the clock the day the paper is sent has nothing to compensate for -- and the practical effect runs against the practitioner: a notice arriving on day three leaves seven days, not ten, and nothing gives the time back. **THIS IS A FIFTH ANSWER TO A QUESTION FOUR STATES ALREADY SPLIT ON** (MS +3, HI +2, ID nothing, NE nothing on the same post-motion trigger) and it is a different KIND of answer: there is no extension to reach the question with.
  **RSA 288:2 IS A SUNDAY RULE ONLY, AND THAT MAKES A FOURTH ANSWER TO ONE DATE.** Rule 2 rolls off "a legal holiday as specified in RSA CH. 288" -- a CHAPTER, where Hawaiʻi's Rule 6(a) named HRS Sec. 8-1 alone. So RSA 288:2 IS inside the reference and IS carried, and it then reads in full: "When any holiday listed in RSA 288:1 falls on SUNDAY, the following day shall be observed as a holiday." **No Saturday limb at all.** 4 July 2026 is a Saturday, so there is no Friday 3 July observance here -- not by interpretation, because no clause could produce one. **Idaho and Nebraska CARRY 3 July 2026** (Saturday-shift clauses inside the very sections their rules cite); **Hawaiʻi omits it as a READING**; **New Hampshire omits it because the rule does not exist.** Asserted directly against the id and ne calendar files. And no RSA 288:1 holiday falls on a Sunday in 2026, checked date by date, so the shift that does exist is dormant this year.
  **THE HOLIDAY LIST IS CLOSED -- NO PROCLAMATION LIMB, WHICH NO OTHER SEEDED STATUTE MANAGES.** Idaho and Hawaiʻi both end with a day appointed by the President or governor, Kansas reaches any day observed by supreme court order, Nebraska subordinates its dates to the federal schedule AND adds a governor limb. RSA 288:1 enumerates and stops: "...and Christmas Day are legal holidays." There is no open-ended gap to disclose. Ten dates for 2026, with New Hampshire's own statutory NAMES -- "Martin Luther King, Jr. CIVIL RIGHTS DAY" and "WASHINGTON'S BIRTHDAY", not Presidents' Day. Columbus Day IS enumerated (Hawaiʻi has none); no Juneteenth, no day after Thanksgiving, no Good Friday.
  **THE ONE JUDGMENT CALL IS THE BIENNIAL ELECTION DAY, AND IT IS OMITTED.** RSA 288:1 makes "the day on which the BIENNIAL ELECTION is held" a legal holiday and 2026 is an even year, so one exists. Dating it needs a second statute that does not use the same word: **RSA 653:7** fixes "the STATE GENERAL ELECTION ... on the first Tuesday following the first Monday in November of every even-numbered year" -- **Tuesday 3 November 2026** -- and RSA ch. 652 defines election, regular election, state election and state general election and **never defines "biennial election"**. Near-certain, still a reading across two chapters. **The direction decides it:** omitting reports EARLY and is safe, carrying a non-holiday reports LATE and loses the filing. Omitted, disclosed, and the date named. Probed as a negative: a period landing on 2026-11-03 does NOT roll.
  **THE PUBLISHED RULES CARRY NO AMENDMENT HISTORY AND NO EFFECTIVE DATES AT ALL** -- checked on the combined page and on an individual rule page, in sharp contrast to HI/ID/NE/NM which all print bracketed notes. So every effective_from comes from the **22 May 2013 adoption order**: "The amendments shall take effect October 1, 2013", plus the rule set's own PREAMBLE in Appendix B ("They take effect on October 1, 2013, and apply to civil actions pending or filed in superior court on or after that date") -- **a preamble the currently-published web version does not reproduce.**
  **AND THE DIFF AGAINST THAT ORDER HAS A LIMIT THAT CHANGES WHAT IT PROVES.** Each row's operative sentence was searched verbatim in the order. **The order's PDF text layer DROPS WORDS** -- Rule 43 comes back missing the eleven words that carry the period. **So a hit proves PRESENCE and a miss proves NOTHING.** Absence had to be established from the order's TABLE OF CONTENTS, which is clean: rules 1 through 54, **no 13A, no 13B, no 28A, no 55**. Two real periods were then dropped for want of a date (the New Mexico discipline): **Rule 13A**'s ten-day reply and three-day clerk notice, and **Rule 12**'s thirty-day summary-judgment objection and twenty-day reply. Both named in the coverage entry so a caller knows not to compute them here. One seeded row DID change wording -- Rule 28(a) said FILE-and-DELIVER in 2013 and says SERVE now, same thirty days -- and the row's note states the change rather than hiding it.
  **ADMISSIONS ARE A PLAIN THIRTY AND THE PROTECTION SITS ON THE OTHER SIDE OF THE TRANSACTION.** MS/NM/SC/HI all make Rule 36 a later_of ("shall not be required ... before the expiration of" 45 days); Kansas makes all three elections; Nebraska's say "whichever is longer". New Hampshire forbids the REQUEST instead: it may be served only "AFTER 30 DAYS AFTER the date the defendant is served with the Summons and Complaint", and the response is then a flat thirty. Same protection, opposite mechanism -- and **NO ROW IN THIS SEED IS A resolve_periods**, the first since Idaho. Rule 28 is self-executing, so a later_of copied across would tell a New Hampshire defendant the matter is still open when it has already been admitted.
  **NO SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION AT ANY LENGTH, AND FOUR SEEDED ROWS ARE TEN DAYS.** Rule 2 is two sentences and has none -- joins MN/UT/NV/KS/ID/NE. Arkansas's 14-day threshold and Alabama's and Wisconsin's 11 would ALL have excluded weekends from those four. Proved as a date: Friday 6 March 2026 + 10 straight days is Monday 16 March, where business-day counting gives Friday the 20th.
  **RULE 43 IS ONE SENTENCE DOING THE WORK OF FOUR FEDERAL RULES**, and it splits on the TRIGGER, not the number: a JURY verdict runs from its RENDITION (the parties were in the room), any OTHER verdict or decree from the DATE ON THE WRITTEN NOTICE (they were not). Same ten days. **The string "new trial" does not occur in the New Hampshire civil rules at all** -- no Rule 50/52/59 equivalent, no motion to alter or amend, no JMOL period. **Rule 2 also has no non-extendable list**, where Hawaiʻi's 6(b) freezes six rules and reaches into the appellate rules; Rule 1(d) instead lets the court waive ANY rule.
  **SOURCE: A 403 IS NOT ALWAYS A WALL, AND THIS IS A FOURTH SHAPE.** courts.nh.gov 403s a plain curl AND WebFetch on every path including the homepage, and returns 200 to the same URL with a full browser header set -- 2,225,391 bytes, 228,837 characters, all 55 rules plus 13A/13B/28A, free, no CAPTCHA, no terms, no login. **It is a header check.** Colorado is a WALL; New Mexico was an OBSTACLE needing a browser pass; Idaho was only wrong guessed URLs. Completeness checked, not assumed: 60 distinct rule numbers extracted.
  **NOT READ, AND RECORDED AS SUCH:** the NHJB's own "Court Holidays - 2026" PDF (posted 11 June 2025, with a 2027 one beside it) refuses every automated route available here. **It is not the legal test in any event, and the reason is the Kansas position inverted:** Rule 2 keys on a day being "a legal holiday as specified in RSA ch. 288", so the legislature's list governs, where K.S.A. 60-206(a)(6) keys on a day being observed BY ORDER OF THE SUPREME COURT and the Judicial Branch's published list therefore IS the test there. A day the New Hampshire courts close that RSA 288:1 does not name is still a countable day under Rule 2.
  **NO BACKWARD ROW IS SEEDED AND THE ONE GIVEN UP IS THE MOST INTERESTING ON THE PLATFORM:** Rule 26(b) requires deposition notice "at least 3 days, EXCLUSIVE OF THE DAY OF SERVICE AND THE DAY OF CAPTION, before the day on which they are to be taken" -- a period carrying its OWN both-endpoints-excluded convention that Rule 2 does not supply.

  **THREE OF THE TEN WERE GENUINELY ABSENT, AND THEY ARE NOT EQUALLY ABSENT.** Chiropractic: **zero occurrences of the word anywhere in the file**. Dialysis: 2, both incidental. Oncology: 6, every one of them an `<option>` in a specialty dropdown. **Chiropractic was the real gap and is the one built.**
  **AND ONE OF THE REMAINING TWO IS SMALLER THAN IT LOOKS.** The live **Drug Administration & J-Code Billing** panel already carries billing-unit math, the JW/JZ waste gate and the infusion hierarchy -- most of what an oncology module would be built out of. Oncology is a real gap but a narrower one than the list implies; **dialysis/ESRD is the cleaner next build**, and neither was started.
  **THE MODULE ITSELF IS SHAPED THE OPPOSITE WAY TO EVERY OTHER SPECIALTY HERE.** Medicare coverage is "specifically limited to treatment by means of manual manipulation, i.e., by use of hands" and **three CPT codes carry the entire benefit**. The office visit, the x-ray, the therapy modality and the supply are excluded **by statute** when a DC performs them -- not denied for necessity, excluded regardless of it. So the useful checks are about what must NOT be on the claim.
  **EVERY EXCLUSION IS GATED ON PAYER AND THAT IS THE PART THAT WOULD HAVE MADE IT WRONG.** A commercial plan routinely covers what Medicare excludes by statute, and most chiropractic volume is not Medicare. Asserted in both directions: the same E/M code blocks for Medicare and stays silent for a commercial payer; so does the manual-device charge.
  **THE PANEL ASKS FOR REGIONS, NOT A REGION COUNT.** The count selects the code (98940 = 1-2, 98941 = 3-4, 98942 = 5), so a count the user types is the one input that cannot be checked against anything. Region NAMES can be -- against the five areas SE1601's own subluxation table enumerates, and against the M99.0x level cited as primary. **An unrecognised region is surfaced and NOT counted**, because silently counting it would change the code.

The two wrong-modifier cases now precede the two missing-modifier cases, and **GA-with-no-ABN was added as its own block**: it is a false attestation of advance notice, not a formatting slip.
  **SOURCES: MLN Matters SE1601 (rev. 7 May 2019) and Billing and Coding Guidelines L34585, both read in full and BOTH 403 A PLAIN FETCH.** WebFetch and plain `curl` are refused; the same URLs return 200 with a full browser header set -- **the identical header-check shape courts.nh.gov has**, now seen on a second unrelated domain. Recorded in the module header so the next reader does not take a 403 for a paywall and fall back to a billing blog. All 16 source strings quote one of those two documents and nothing else, **asserted mechanically** rather than by inspection.

  **CHAPTER 8 NEVER NAMES A SINGLE 909xx MCP CODE, WHICH IS THE THING THAT SHAPED THE BUILD.** CMS Pub. 100-04 Ch. 8 (Rev. 13740, issued 17 Apr 2026, downloaded and read in full) states the whole policy -- age band, visit band, home vs centre, per-day -- and leaves the codes to CPT. The descriptors that fix each code came from the **California Medi-Cal provider manual**, a government publication that reproduces them. **Two sources, and which one carries which claim is stated on the finding rather than blurred into one citation.**
  **THIRD DOCUMENT IN TWO DAYS TO 403 A PLAIN FETCH AND RETURN 200 TO A BROWSER HEADER SET** -- courts.nh.gov, then SE1601/L34585, now cms.gov's manual downloads. This is no longer a curiosity; **treat a 403 from a government site as a header check until proven otherwise.**
  **THE MODULE DELEGATES INSTEAD OF DUPLICATING, WHICH WAS THE EXPLICIT INSTRUCTION AND IS ALSO THE RIGHT CALL.** `evaluateJwJz()` in the Drug Administration module already carries an `esrd` branch and the AY note that belongs with it, so the waste-modifier question is passed to it; `calcDrugBillingUnits()` is pointed at rather than copied. **The harness extracts `evaluateJwJz` FROM `sairncode.html` and prepends it**, so a stub cannot make the delegation test pass while the wiring is broken -- and the live run extracted it from the file Vercel serves. The JW/JZ policy has already moved three times (JW 1 Jan 2017, JZ 1 Jul 2023, unprocessable returns 1 Oct 2023); one copy is hard enough to keep current.
  **BUNDLED IS THE DEFAULT AND SEPARATE PAYMENT IS THE EXCEPTION.** One per-treatment PPS payment covers supplies, equipment, drugs, biologicals, labs, training and support services, and consolidated billing edits **deny or reject** another provider's claim for the same item. That is what makes AY load-bearing rather than clerical, and it is checked **in both directions**: AY on an ESRD-related item misstates the claim just as surely as its absence forfeits payment on an unrelated one.
  **A FINDING WORTH RECORDING ON ITS OWN: CHAPTER 8 SECTION 170.A STILL INSTRUCTS BILLING 90919-90925.** Verified against Rev. 13740 itself -- the revision current today, issued six weeks ago. **Those codes are deleted.** A biller following the manual literally produces a deleted code. They are blocked, and the block names the section that prescribes them, because that is the part a coder cannot find on their own. **No deletion DATE is asserted** -- FindACode says 2009-01-01, the crosswalk evidence points at a 2004 transition to G-codes, and neither is primary, so the module says the codes are deleted and stops there.
  **FEBRUARY PRODUCES NO NUMBER, AND A TEST FAILS IF ONE IS EVER INVENTED.** The manual states 13 treatments for a 30-day month and 14 for a 31-day month and gives **no figure for a 28- or 29-day month**. Deriving one would have been trivial and would have been fabrication. The check says the manual is silent and states the underlying 3-per-week policy instead.
  Also encoded, all sourced: age band taken from the age at the **end** of the month; centre-based 4+/2-3/1 visit bands with **zero visits having no code at all**; home dialysis as a single monthly rate that does not vary with visit count but needs one face-to-face visit, waivable case by case; **a month containing ANY home dialysis takes the home code**, in either direction and at any proportion; one MCP per calendar month across every physician; monthly and per-day never together; per-day units equal to days of responsibility and per-day codes confined to the four named circumstances, never a full month; **inpatient visits excluded from the count while observation and SNF-resident visits count**; 90935/90937/90945/90947 payable only at POS 21, one per day, same-day E/M needing modifier 25; the TDAPA rules including the **AX modifier's 1 July 2026 boundary**, which is live law that changed eight weeks ago; and the 13/14 limit with the CG attestation, where CG inside the limit is blocked because it gives away payment for nothing.

  **IT IS THE NARROW MODULE ON PURPOSE, WHICH IS WHY IT WAS RANKED LAST.** The Drug Administration panel already does J-code unit arithmetic, the JW/JZ gate, NDC format, the biosimilar families and the initial-code hierarchy, and every one of those is oncology work.   **THE REUSE IS WIRED, NOT NOMINAL, AND THE HARNESS PROVES IT.** The service checkboxes render from the same `DU_INFUSION_SERVICES` array the Drug Administration panel renders from, and the initial code comes from calling `determineInitialInfusionCode()`. The test **extracts both from `sairncode.html`** and prepends them, so a stub cannot make the wiring look live. Same discipline as the ESRD module's `evaluateJwJz` delegation.
  **THE SINGLE MOST VALUABLE RULE IN THE MODULE IS ONE SENTENCE.** Ch. 12 sec. 30.5.D: *"The administration of anti-anemia drugs and anti-emetic drugs by injection or infusion for cancer patients is not considered chemotherapy administration."* **No amount of care about the chemo > therapeutic > hydration hierarchy catches this, because the drug is not in the hierarchy at all.** The inverse is encoded too and is just as counter-intuitive: an anti-neoplastic given for a **non-cancer** diagnosis still uses the chemotherapy family -- CMS's own example is cyclophosphamide for an auto-immune condition.
  **EVERY THRESHOLD IS PINNED TO CMS'S OWN WORKED EXAMPLES RATHER THAN TRUSTED.** The additional-hour rule -- reportable only where the interval is **GREATER** than 30 minutes beyond the one-hour increment -- is asserted against CMS's 1h45m example plus boundary tests at 90 and 91 minutes. The radiation weekly arithmetic is asserted against **all four** of CMS's examples: 18 fractions = 4 weekly services, 62 = 12, 8 = 2, 6 = 1.
  **AND WHERE THE MANUAL STOPS, THE MODULE SAYS SO.** CMS states the 30-minute threshold **once**, with **one** worked example, for the **first** additional hour. Applying it to each further increment is that same rule applied again, not a separately stated one -- so the output discloses that in as many words, with **a test that fails if the disclosure is dropped and a second that fails if it is attached to a case the example already covers.** Same shape as February getting no ESRD treatment limit.
  **OFF-LABEL COVERAGE HAS THREE OUTCOMES, NOT TWO,** and the third is the manual's own: the complete absence of narrative text on a use is **neither supportive nor non-supportive**. **NCCN Category 2B falls in neither list** -- the manual names 1 and 2A as accepted and 3 as not -- so no answer is asserted for it. Each compendium also carries **the date it became recognised**, because recognition is not retroactive: AHFS-DI was already listed, NCCN/DrugDex/Clinical Pharmacology were added in 2008 and Lexi-Drugs in 2015, and a use supported only by Lexi-Drugs on a 2014 date of service was not supported then.

Three box-drawing characters are Guardian Check 6's documented false positive, all in `//` comments, 0a passing.
  **ONE WORKFLOW NOTE WORTH KEEPING:** a `git commit -m "..."` whose message contains a **double quote** breaks out of the Bash string and git reads the remaining words as **pathspecs** -- `error: pathspec 'complete' did not match any file(s)`. It failed safe (nothing was committed, the staged file stayed staged) but the failure mode reads like a file error rather than a quoting one. Write the message to a file and use `git commit -F` whenever it quotes a source. 2026-09-01
- api/_lib/deadline-engine.js + api/_lib/deadline-weekend-days.test.js + docs/SAIRN-OPEN-WORK-INDEX.md (Hank, 2026-09-01) -- **PER-JURISDICTION WEEKEND DAYS BUILT, PUSHED AND LIVE (`cc68b87`).** Picked from the SAIRNlaw vertical over seeding a 36th state because it is the change Michael explicitly prioritised on 2026-08-26 and because **a wrong answer outranks a missing one**.
  **THE ASSUMPTION THAT WAS HARDCODED.** `isWeekend()` returned true for Saturday and Sunday and `rollOff()` rolled both, unconditionally, for every jurisdiction. **Louisiana is the first candidate where that is affirmatively wrong:** La. C.C.P. art. 5059(A) rolls the last day only "unless it is a legal holiday" and **never names a weekend day in its own right**, while La. R.S. 1:55(A)(1) makes **Sundays** a statewide legal holiday and "the whole of every Saturday" one **only** in Orleans parish, the city of Baton Rouge, the parishes of the 2nd and 6th congressional districts except Ascension, and those of the 14th and 31st judicial districts. Everywhere else a Saturday is an ordinary day, and rolling off it reports **LATE** -- the direction that misses a filing, and one a coverage disclosure cannot repair.
  **THE CHANGE:** `weekend_days` on the computation standard, defaulting to `[Sunday, Saturday]`, threaded through **all eight** call sites that consult a weekend day -- `rollOff`, `countExcludingWeekendsAndHolidays`, the short-period exclusion, the shifted-start rollover, the business-days loop, the court-days extension loop, the post-extension rollover, and `applyServiceCompletion`. **No shipped standard declares it**, so all 35 live jurisdictions are unchanged.
  **A MALFORMED DECLARATION THROWS AT MODULE LOAD RATHER THAN FALLING BACK.** Ignoring a bad value and using the default would silently restore the exact behaviour a jurisdiction declared wrong. **And "fails safe" was not available**, which is the second reason it refuses: a missing roll makes a FORWARD deadline earlier (safe) and a BACKWARD one later (unsafe), so no fallback is safe in both directions. That asymmetry is written into the code comment, not just decided once.
  **THE FALSE-GREEN TRAP THE ROW WARNED ABOUT WAS REAL.** All 54 existing suites pass a no-op stub, because the default makes them pass either way -- so a green bar proves nothing here. `api/_lib/deadline-weekend-days.test.js` is the file that does not: **56 assertions, every one a CONTRAST** between a Sunday-only set and the default on the same input. A period landing on Saturday 4 July stops on the Saturday under `[Sun]` and rolls to Monday under the default; **a period landing on Sunday still rolls under both**, so the change is a narrowing and not a disabling; a holiday still rolls regardless; and backward periods, shifted starts, short-period exclusion and business-day counting each get the same paired treatment.
  **IT ALSO AUDITS THE ENGINE SOURCE, because a dynamic test only covers the paths it reaches.** Every `isWeekend` / `rollOff` / `countExcludingWeekendsAndHolidays` call site must pass a weekend argument, asserted by parsing the file with comments stripped. **Proved non-vacuous by a negative control**: removing the argument from ONE `rollOff` call site fails five assertions and the audit names the offending call site verbatim. The negative control also confirmed the file's failure actually surfaces under `node --test` rather than exiting green.
  **ONE FIXTURE BUG WORTH RECORDING BECAUSE IT WOULD HAVE PASSED VACUOUSLY.** Calendar entries are OBJECTS carrying `date`/`name`/`kind`, not bare date strings -- `holidayFor` reads `.date` and consults `.kind` for the FRCP 6(a)(6) state-holiday rule. My first fixture used bare strings, which reads as "no holidays at all" **with no error**, and would have made all three holiday-still-rolls assertions pass while proving nothing. Caught because those three failed for the opposite reason first.
  **LOUISIANA IS STILL NOT SEEDED AND NO `la` STANDARD WAS ADDED.** That is the remaining open half and it is a **decision, not a build**: statewide `la` with `weekend_days [Sun]` is correct for the majority of parishes and LATE for the enumerated ones, and congressional-district membership is **redistricted**, so it is not stable over time either.
  **VERIFIED: 161 tests across the 50 files the glob runs plus the 4 roofing files it misses (111 more) -- 272 assertions, 0 failing.** Live: deployment `dpl_6aDUvL8W5BEGAstXFT6SXFCCbZZd` for `cc68b87` is **READY in production, read from the Vercel API rather than inferred from a clean push** -- which mattered here, because the change is a deliberate no-op for every live jurisdiction and therefore **cannot be distinguished from the old code by behaviour**. A live New Hampshire 30-day period from 2026-06-04 lands on Saturday 4 July and still returns Monday 2026-07-06.
  **TWO SAIRNlaw INDEX ROWS FOUND STALE AND CORRECTED (`42b4d95`).** The coverage cell said 24 jurisdictions / 269 rules; live is **35 / 397**, and North Carolina, Utah, Nevada, Alabama and Wisconsin are all marked as pending in rows below while all five are live -- confirmed jurisdiction by jurisdiction against the endpoint, not inferred from seed files existing. **And the exclusive-vs-combined engine row appears already built**: the engine carries `service_methods`, `exclusivityOf()` and rule-level `requires_exclusive` with an unknown-exclusivity branch, and the Utah and Florida seeds carry 8 and 4 occurrences. Found incidentally, so the correction states plainly what was NOT verified -- who built it, when, or whether the Florida `+5` arithmetic decision was ever taken. 2026-09-01

Code Sec. 1-3-8(f)(1) lets a state office **stay OPEN on a state holiday on sixty days' notice**; if a court does that and the engine rolls off the day anyway, the date is **LATER than the true deadline**. Alabama now carries `direction: 'late'` plus a structured `late_exposure` block -- **authority / summary / why_not_refused / caller_action** -- and the header documents **DISCLOSED-LATE** as a deliberate third category.
  **`direction` IS WORST-CASE, NOT TYPICAL, AND THE CODE SAYS SO.** Two of Alabama's three gaps are EARLY and safe; the field still reads `late`, because a caller switching on it is asking *can this be late* and for Alabama the answer is yes. The two resolved the same problem differently and the text now says how -- **Wisconsin by intersecting to the three days every county closes** (under-inclusive, therefore EARLY); **Alabama cannot**, because its exposure is a discretionary notice provision rather than a published schedule to intersect.
  **VERIFIED: 167 tests pass across `api/_lib`, 0 failing.** Sixteen suites needed their hardcoded coverage key list extended and Massachusetts its per-state regex map -- **the same mechanism that caught this morning's unilateral attempt**, working as designed. **Live: `ut` and `nv` now return `coverage.direction: 'early'` where they previously returned none; `al` returns `'late'` with `late_exposure` present.** 2026-09-01

  **THE SCOPE IS THE HEADLINE.** Del. Ct. Ch. R. 6 is a **different computation on the same subject** -- Register in Chancery inaccessibility as a SEPARATE limb rather than a replacement, no Chief Justice limb in its holiday definition, backward periods addressed expressly, an hours unit -- agreeing with Superior Court **only on the 11-day threshold**. Chancery's own Rules 12/33/34/36 were not read. So the coverage summary **leads with "SUPERIOR COURT ONLY"**, every row's note repeats it, and the test asserts the summary starts with it.
  **ELEVEN IS LOAD-BEARING ON HALF THE SEED.** Five of ten rows are TEN-day Rule 59(b) periods, so the intermediate-day exclusion fires on them: 1 June + 10 is **Monday 15 June**, where straight calendar counting gives Thursday the 11th -- **four days EARLY**. Hand-computed from the rule before being asserted; the negative control confirms it by failing four assertions at a threshold of 7.
  **THE GENERAL ELECTION DAY IS CARRIED -- the OPPOSITE call to New Hampshire's, six weeks after it, and for a principled reason.** There the statute's term was undefined and dating it needed a second statute using a different one, so it was omitted as EARLY/safe. Here **Del. Const. art. V § 1** fixes the general election "biennially on the Tuesday next after the first Monday in the month of November" and **§ 501(a)(12) uses the same term** -- one reference, no gap. **The identification is a citation rather than a reading**, so it is carried. Probed as a positive.
  **SUSSEX COUNTY RETURN DAY IS OMITTED AND IS UNSAFE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS IF MODELLED NAIVELY** -- county-scoped like Suffolk County AND a **half day beginning at noon**, so carrying it statewide as a full rolling day reports LATE. Probed as a negative: 5 November 2026 must not roll.
  **RULES 33 AND 34 CARRY A DEFENDANT ELECTION AND RULE 36 DOES NOT**, all three read rather than the third inferred. "May serve within 45 days" is an election between two triggers, seeded as the 30-day limb (EARLY/safe, Hawaii's own treatment). **Rule 36 is SELF-EXECUTING**, so a floor copied onto it would not merely produce a late date -- it would tell a defendant a matter is still open when it has already been admitted. Asserted as a field: **no row is a resolve_periods**.
  **NO MAIL EXTENSION ON THE ANSWER-TO-PROCESS ROW, deliberately**, because whether Rule 6(e) reaches service of PROCESS is an **open platform question** raised against the us-federal answer row. Omitting reports EARLY; carrying reports three days LATE. The row's note is the record.

  **THE GAP IT CLOSES.** `computeDeadline()` counted from whatever date it was handed and **never asked what that date was**. For 48 rows the trigger is a term of art naming one document, and **four states start the appeal clock four different ways** -- Texas from the **SIGNING** of the judgment, Florida from its **RENDITION**, six jurisdictions from its **ENTRY** on the docket, New York from **SERVICE** of a copy with written notice of entry. **West Virginia uses two different documents inside one state**: R. App. P. 5(b) runs from the final order OR the mandate, 5(f) from the judgment.
  **TWO BEHAVIOURS, BECAUSE THE TWO CLASSES FAIL IN OPPOSITE DIRECTIONS.** An EARLIER document's date reports EARLY (safe); a LATER one reports **LATE**, and 28 of the declared rows are appellate where a late notice of appeal is **jurisdictional and not curable**. So appellate **REFUSES** and civil **WARNS** -- the Kentucky rule applied at row level.
  **`on_unconfirmed` IS WRITTEN INTO EACH ROW, NOT DERIVED FROM THE DOMAIN AT RUNTIME.** The domain generated the values, but a default computed from another field is exactly the invisible rule this change exists to remove -- and one day a civil row will need to refuse.
  **THE REFUSAL RUNS BEFORE ANY ARITHMETIC, AND A TEST ASSERTS THE REFUSAL CARRIES NO `due_date`.** A guard that fired after the date was computed would leave a date in the response for someone to read, which is the whole thing being prevented.

  **FOUR ROWS ARE DELIBERATELY NOT COVERED AND THAT IS THE HONEST REMAINING GAP:** `ny-cplr-5513c-cross-appeal`, `oh-appr-4B1-cross-appeal`, `tx-trap-261d-cross-appeal-ordinary` and `frap-4b1A-criminal-notice-of-appeal` are **multi-trigger** rules whose limbs each carry their own event, so one declaration cannot describe them and a wrong one would be worse than none. **Cross-appeals are exactly the rows most likely to be conflated**, since they run from the other party's notice.   **VERIFIED: 41 mechanism assertions on synthetic rules** (so the mechanism is proven independently of any jurisdiction's data), **170 tests across `api/_lib`, 0 failing**, load-state 407 rules / 143 calendars MISSING 0 STALE 0 EXTRA 0. 2026-09-02

Live-verified on Texas 26.1(d): unconfirmed → refuse, correct → **2026-07-09 `confirmed`**, wrong document → mismatch, and **confirming the OTHER limb does not unlock the guarded one**.
  **THE PART WORTH KEEPING: PROVING THE GUARD'S PLACEMENT TOOK TWO ATTEMPTS, AND THE FIRST TEST WAS DECORATIVE.** The design warned that a guard placed beside its singular sibling would "return the right refusal code while having already done the arithmetic". My first assertion for that was **"the refusal carries no `due_date`"** -- which is **true of BOTH placements**, because a misplaced guard still returns early and the computed limb dates are simply discarded. **A scratch copy with the guard deliberately moved passed the file 78/78.** That is precisely the broken-but-green shape the design was written to avoid, produced by the test meant to prevent it.

Re-run against the misplaced copy it fails 8 assertions across all four rows. **Live-verified too:** one limb date omitted still returns the document refusal.

Loaded: MISSING 0 STALE 0 EXTRA 0. **171 tests, 0 failing.** 2026-09-02
- sairnlaw.html + api/_lib/sairnlaw-trust-void-race.test.js (Hank, 2026-09-02) -- **TED'S HIGHEST-VALUE UNCLAIMED HIT CHECKED: THE REPORTED SHAPE WAS A FALSE POSITIVE, AND THERE WAS A REAL TRUST-MONEY BUG UNDER IT (`aa77a99`). Live-verified 8/8.**
  **THE REPORTED read-await-write STALE-ARRAY PATTERN IS NOT PRESENT.** Both `confirmVoid` and `saveTrustTransaction` **re-read** the collection after the await, so an unrelated transaction changed or added during the round trip survives untouched -- cases 5 and 6 of the harness pass against the **original** code. Same outcome as the two reported SAIRNlegacy hits: the scanner sees a key written from two variables and cannot tell a lost update from a correct re-read. **`saveTrustTransaction` is clean and needed no change.**
  **WHAT WAS REAL IS THE SAME FAMILY POINTING THE OTHER WAY, and the re-read is what made it easy to miss.** `confirmVoid`'s rejection path re-read the list and then set the record back to `Posted` **unconditionally**. If another device voided the SAME transaction while the request was in flight, and the server rejected this request for any reason other than `ALREADY_VOIDED` -- the deposit-void balance guard, for instance -- **the revert resurrected a void that is genuinely on the server** and wiped the other void's reason and timestamp with it.
  **THE MONEY CONSEQUENCE IS NOT ABSTRACT.** `clientLedgerBalance` counts **Posted** rows, so a resurrected **Deposit** inflates a client's trust balance -- and that balance is exactly what `saveTrustTransaction`'s disbursement guard checks against. **The failure direction is authorising a disbursement that overdraws real client funds**, which is the one thing trust accounting exists to prevent.
  **THE SAME TRAP APPLIED BEFORE THE CLICK.** The Void button renders only on a `Posted` row, but the row comes from a snapshot and another device can void it between render and click. `confirmVoid` then re-voided it, overwriting the real reason and timestamp; a later rejection set it to Posted. It now **refuses an already-voided transaction** and says so.
  **THE FIX MIRRORS SAIRNLEGACY'S:** the revert fires only if the record still carries **THIS call's** void, identified by the timestamp this call wrote. A different one means someone else's newer state, which is more correct than the one being rolled back to. **When the revert is skipped the user is told**, because silently leaving a rejected void in place would read as success.
  **PROVED IN BOTH DIRECTIONS BY DRIVING THE REAL FUNCTION** -- extracted verbatim from `sairnlaw.html`, run with the concurrent change happening **inside** the await. **5/8 against the pre-fix code, 8/8 after**, and 8/8 again against the file **Vercel actually serves**.
  **AND THE HARNESS IS COMMITTED THIS TIME.** The SAIRNlegacy fix kept its eight race checks in a scratchpad. **This class has now been found in four apps in one night** -- SAIRNlegacy, SAIRNcare's eMAR, SAIRNcode's settings save, and here -- **and nothing permanent guarded any of them.** Trust money is the wrong place to keep that precedent, so `api/_lib/sairnlaw-trust-void-race.test.js` runs in the standing suite. 172 tests, 0 failing; sairnlaw.html 2/2 blocks, 261 globals 0 duplicates, 0 missing DOM targets, nav PASS, divs balanced, CRLF preserved, 0 stray CRs. 2026-09-02

  **THE HIGHEST-VALUE FINDING IS AN ABSENCE, AND IT WOULD HAVE BEEN INVISIBLE TO A COPY.** Mont. R. Civ. P. 6(a)(1)(B) counts *"every day, including intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays"* -- **Montana has NO short-period exclusion threshold at all.** Delaware, gated the day before under a rule also numbered 6(a), carries **11**; Ohio and Indiana 7; Arkansas 14. **Five of the fifteen rows are shorter than 15 days** and every one of them would have moved LATER under a borrowed number. Proven on a real row: 14 days from Monday 1 June 2026 is **15 June** counting straight and **19 June** excluding intermediate weekends -- four days, in the direction that loses a filing.
  **THE SECOND FINDING IS THE ONE THE FAMILY RESEMBLANCE HIDES: `frcp_6d` IS THE WRONG STANDARD.** Mont. R. Civ. P. 6(d) extends for service under **Rule 5(b)(2)(C), (D), (E) or (F)** -- the **pre-2016** federal set. **FRCP 6(d) dropped (E), electronic service, in 2016.** So a Montana party served by consented e-mail gets three days and a federal one does not; reusing `frcp_6d` would have silently dropped three days from every consented electronic service. `mt_rcp_6d` is its own standard and the suite asserts the two **disagree** on the same method rather than asserting Montana alone.
  **THE SCOPING TRAP IS SHARPER THAN DELAWARE'S CHANCERY AND SITS IN THE SAME TITLE OF THE SAME CODE.** `Mont. Just. & City Ct. R. Civ. P. 6` (MCA Title 25 **ch. 23**) is **also numbered Rule 6** and is a different computation: it defines "legal holiday" **not at all**, so nothing narrows MCA 1-1-216 the way District Court Rule 6(a)(6) does; no clerk-inaccessibility limb; no backward "next day" rule; no hours unit; and its extension is **mail only** and reads *"3 days must be added to the prescribed period"* -- **the opposite sequencing** to the District Court rule's *"added AFTER the period would otherwise expire"*. Seeded DISTRICT COURT ONLY and said so in the readme, the coverage entry, the standard's comment and every row's note.
  **PRESIDENTS' DAY WAS DATED, NOT GUESSED, AND THE METHOD IS THE REUSABLE PART.** Rule 6(a)(6)(A) still reaches *"the day set aside by statute for observing ... **Lincoln's and Washington's Birthdays**"*. The current statute says *"Presidents' Day"*. **Found by pulling the 2011 and 2023 editions of MCA 1-1-216 rather than reading the current one**: both said "Lincoln's and Washington's Birthdays, the third Monday in February", and **Ch. 561, L. 2025 (SB 224) -- the Indigenous Peoples' Day bill -- renamed the entry and moved nothing.** The rule's list was never conformed. **CARRIED IN BOTH DIRECTIONS, EACH FOR ITS OWN REASON:** forward it is certain twice over, because **Rule 6(a)(6)(C)** independently reaches *"any other day declared a holiday by the state"* **for periods measured after an event**; backward limb (C) is unavailable by its own terms, and **carrying is the SAFE direction there** -- a backward period that fails to roll reports LATE. That inverts the usual calculus and is why it needed working out.
  **THE SAME TWO-EDITION METHOD SETTLED THE SATURDAY SHIFT.** MCA 1-1-216(2)(b) -- Saturday holiday, preceding Friday -- came in with **Ch. 131, L. 2013**, not 2025; the 2011 text has a Sunday limb only. In force for every year this platform computes. Carried because Rule 6(a)(6)(A) reaches the day set aside **for OBSERVING** the holiday, an observance reference rather than a section or chapter reference -- **a fifth distinct route to Friday 3 July 2026** alongside Idaho/Nebraska (clause in the cited section), Delaware/Maryland (clause in the cited chapter), Hawaii (clause outside the reference, omitted) and New Hampshire (no Saturday limb exists).
  **THE ELECTION DAY IS THE SHORTEST REACH YET: THREE PROVISIONS, ONE TERM.** Rule 6(a)(6)(A) names *"state general election day"*, MCA 1-1-216(1)(l) uses the same three words, MCA 13-1-104(1) dates it. Tuesday **3 November 2026**. Delaware's needed the state constitution; New Hampshire's was **omitted** because its statute names a term the election code never defines.
  **RULES 33/34 CARRY AN ELECTION AND RULE 36 CARRIES A FLOOR -- THE EXACT INVERSE OF DELAWARE**, which has an election on 33 and 34 and **nothing** on 36. All three read in full; none inferred from the other two. Rule 36 is **self-executing**, so a flat thirty would tell a defendant a matter is still open **fifteen days after it was admitted**. Seeded `resolve_periods: later_of` and probed with **each limb winning in turn** (30-limb to 2026-07-01, 45-limb floor to 2026-07-16).
  **TWO BACKWARD ROWS, WHICH DELAWARE COULD NOT HAVE.** Rule 6(a)(5) addresses the direction expressly. Probed on a 7-day count from a 1 June hearing that must roll **BACKWARD** off **Memorial Day** and then past the weekend behind it, to **Friday 22 May** -- rolling forward would land on the 26th and give the opposing party fewer than the seven days the rule guarantees.
  **TWO OMISSIONS COST COVERAGE AND ARE DISCLOSED RATHER THAN QUIET, both EARLY.** Rule 36 carries **no service extension**: its 45-day limb runs from Rule 4 service that Rule 6(d) does not reach, and **this engine extends per ROW, not per LIMB**, so carrying it would add three unauthorised days exactly when the floor governs. Rule 12(a)(4)(A) carries none either -- its period runs from *notice of the court's action*, not from service. **Filed, not acted on: Hawaii's equivalent Rule 36 row DOES carry its extension**, and whether Haw. R. Civ. P. 6(e) is gated on a service subsection the way Montana's expressly is **was not read**, so that row is not changed on the strength of this one.
  **ALSO RECORDED AS A DECISION RATHER THAN A DEFAULT:** Rule 6(b)(2) forbids the court to extend 59(b), (d) and (e) -- no good cause, no excusable neglect -- which is an argument for setting those rows' `trigger_document` guard to `refuse`. They are set to **`warn`**, matching Delaware's identical row and the platform's civil/appellate convention. The alternative is written into the row and the gate doc so it is a choice, not an oversight.
  **NEGATIVE CONTROL RUN BEFORE THE SUITE WAS TRUSTED, four ways, files restored byte-identical afterwards:** giving Montana Delaware's 11-day exclusion fails **4** assertions, dropping the consented-electronic limb fails **3**, flipping the 7-day row to forward fails **4**, adding Delaware's day-after-Thanksgiving fails **4**. Unbroken: **80/80**.

Split into two commits and pushed the code-only one **from a detached HEAD**, where no Montana seed exists on disk, so live == repo was TRUE rather than bypassed. That is the New Hampshire precedent from 2026-09-01, applied deliberately. **`SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off` was NOT used.** First load attempt confirmed the deploy had not landed (`computation must be one of ...`); the re-run loaded all 15 rules and 1 calendar, and only then was the seed commit pushed -- gate silent.

- sairnroofing.html (Hank, 2026-09-02) -- **CROSS-APP read-await-write / UTC-DATE SWEEP CLOSED OUT. `3d65fa9`. Of the eight unclaimed hits carried in the handoff, SEVEN WERE FALSE POSITIVES OR ALREADY FIXED and ONE was real -- SAIRNroofing, four sites, three of them stored record dates. Live-verified against the deployed file.**
  **THE REAL ONE: SAIRNROOFING HAD NO LOCAL-DATE HELPER AT ALL**, so there was nothing to reach for and four call sites derived the day from `toISOString()`. **NOT COSMETIC, because the server keeps what the client sends** -- `api/_lib/roofing-billing.js` validates `issued_on`, `decided_on` and `received_on` as YYYY-MM-DD, never recomputes the day, and then **SORTS proposal history by `issued_on` and picks the latest decision by `decided_on`**. So the wrong day is stored, displayed, exported and ordered on. `rfIssueProposal` recorded a Monday-evening proposal as issued Tuesday; `rfRecordDecision` defaulted the **acceptance date on a signed roofing agreement**; `rfPromptPayment` recorded an end-of-day **cheque** as received the next day.
  **PROVED BOTH WAYS BY DRIVING THE REAL FUNCTION**, `rfLocalToday()` extracted verbatim from the file and run against the expression it replaced with the clock pinned and `TZ=America/New_York`: **3 of 5 probes had the old value WRONG** -- 9:30pm and 11:59pm on Mon 1 Jun 2026 both gave 2026-06-02, and **9:00pm on Wed 31 December 2025 gave 2026-01-01, recording a payment in the following TAX YEAR.** The two after-midnight probes agree, which is what makes the first three a real difference rather than a broken harness.
  **THE TWO BARE INSTANTS WERE LEFT ALONE ON PURPOSE** -- `assessed_at` on a damage assessment and `rescinded_at` on a cancelled agreement are moments, not calendar days. Rewriting those is the opposite error and the scanner already declines to report them.
  **LIVE-VERIFIED, NOT ASSUMED FROM THE PUSH:** fetched `sairn.vercel.app/sairnroofing` after the deploy landed -- **`rfLocalToday` defined, all 4 call sites present, and the scanner run against the LIVE FILE reports 0 UTC calendar derivations, helper present, 0 stale-snapshot hits.** The one raw regex match left in the live file is the text of the fix's own comment, which is exactly the false positive `strip_comments()` exists to remove.
  **THE SEVEN THAT WERE NOT DEFECTS, each read individually rather than trusted from the scanner's silence** -- the tightened scanner drops them all, and a tightening can introduce false NEGATIVES, so the named functions were opened:
  * **`sairndesign.saveInvoice`** -- FALSE POSITIVE. Its 409 rollback does `list=invoices().filter(...)`, a **re-read after the await**; the optimistic insert is written before any await. Already the corrected shape.

  **THE GAP WAS A CONTRADICTION INSIDE ONE APP, ONE PANEL APART, AND THE WEX PLUMBING WAS ALREADY FINE.** `api/_lib/wex.js`, `/api/legal-reference` and the Legal Reference panel were built correctly and are **live-verified working**: `res judicata` and `promissory estoppel` both return real retrieved text with a real `law.cornell.edu/wex/...` URL, a nonexistent term returns **NOT_FOUND** with the honest message, and a second request inside ten seconds returns **RATE_LIMITED** because the published `Crawl-delay: 10` is genuinely honoured deployment-wide. **Nothing in the retrieval layer needed fixing.** What needed fixing was one panel over.
  **THE AI ASSISTANT HAD TWO TOOLS -- `get_matters` and `get_deadlines` -- AND NEITHER WAS THE GROUNDED ONE**, while its system prompt ended *"You may still help with general drafting, EXPLAINING CONCEPTS, and non-firm-specific questions."* So *"what is res judicata?"* was answered from **model memory, unsourced**, in the same product whose Legal Reference screen says *"Nothing on this screen is answered from the model's memory."*
  **NEITHER EXISTING GUARD REACHED IT, WHICH IS WHY IT SURVIVED SIX WEEKS OF HARDENING.** `LAW_CITATION_RULE` forbids emitting a citation STRING and **expressly ALLOWS narrative description**; `LAW_FIRM_DATA_RULE` guards this firm's own data. A definition is neither of those things. **A rule that is carefully written about one failure mode is not evidence about a neighbouring one** -- that is the transferable part.
  **THREE CHANGES, AND THEY ONLY WORK TOGETHER:** `define_legal_term` registered against the SAME endpoint the panel uses and composing nothing; `lawExecuteTool` made **async** because the lookup is a network call; and `LAW_DEFINITION_RULE` spliced into the system prompt with the *"explaining concepts"* clause narrowed so the instruction and the mechanism stop contradicting each other. **The test asserts the returned key set EXACTLY**, so a future "helpful" extra field fails rather than ships.
  **THE OLD SYNCHRONOUS DISPATCHER WOULD HAVE FAILED SILENTLY, AND ITS OWN COMMENT HAD PREDICTED THE SHAPE WITHOUT EVER MEASURING IT.** It read: *"an async run() ... would silently report {ok:true, result:&lt;pending Promise&gt;}"*. **Measured, it is worse than that: `JSON.stringify(pending Promise)` is `"{}"`,** so `sendAI()` would have put an **EMPTY OBJECT** into `tool_result` with `ok:true` and nothing would have thrown, logged, or looked wrong. **Asserted directly against a reconstructed copy of the old dispatcher**, so the async change is proven load-bearing rather than claimed to be. **A correct warning that nobody had turned into a test is still an untested claim.**
  **SAIRNBIZ'S `sbExecuteTool` KEEPS THE SYNC-ONLY CONSTRAINT AND WAS NOT TOUCHED** -- it has no async tool, and changing another app's dispatcher on the strength of this one's need is the unrequested spread that makes a diff hard to review. The divergence is written into both places so the next reader does not file it as drift.
  **THE REFUSAL REASON IS CARRIED THROUGH ON PURPOSE, VIA AN OPT-IN `userSafe` FLAG.** RATE_LIMITED is normal and **retryable**; NOT_FOUND is **permanent**. Flattening both into "could not retrieve that data" makes the retryable one look final and the final one look like a glitch. Anything NOT marked `userSafe` is still flattened -- asserted in both directions, including that a thrown `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY missing` **does not reach the user**. Refusals route through the `ok:false` branch specifically so they pick up `sendAI()`'s existing anti-substitution reinforcement in the same turn -- the mechanism already proven for a denied `get_matters`, reused rather than rebuilt.
  **NEGATIVE CONTROL, file restored byte-identical afterwards:** unwiring the rule from the prompt fails **1**, letting the tool compose a *"broadly speaking"* fallback fails **4**, flattening the refusal reasons fails **4**, and reverting the dispatcher to synchronous **stops the suite RUNNING at all** -- the extractor requires `async function lawExecuteTool`, so that one is a hard stop rather than a clean assertion, **stated plainly rather than counted as an assertion it is not.**
  **DELIBERATELY NOT DONE, AND IT IS A REAL DECISION RATHER THAN AN OVERSIGHT: the two other AI surfaces in this file were left alone.** Document drafting (`sairnlaw.html:3008`) and document review (`:3055`) carry `LAW_CITATION_RULE` but **not** the definition rule. **Adding it there would be actively wrong: both are single-shot `ai_generate` calls that send NO `tools` field at all**, so a rule ordering them to call `define_legal_term` would instruct them to use a tool they cannot reach -- and drafting a contract clause that *defines* a term is legitimate drafting, which that prompt already protects. **Flagged for Michael rather than decided unilaterally:** if those surfaces should also be barred from asserting what a term means, the fix is a different one (a prohibition without a tool, or giving them tools), not a copy of this rule.
  **HONEST LIMIT, the same one the citation rule states about itself:** no system prompt makes a fabricated definition structurally impossible for any LLM. What changed today is that **the instruction and the mechanism agree**. Before, only the instruction existed and it said the opposite.

- api/_lib/deadline-engine.js (READ-ONLY MEASUREMENT, NOTHING CHANGED) (Hank, 2026-09-02) -- **THE HAWAII QUESTION IS ANSWERED AND IT IS NOT A HAWAII QUESTION. 14 OF 15 MULTI-TRIGGER ROWS CARRYING A SERVICE EXTENSION APPLY IT TO A LIMB NO MAIL RULE REACHES, ACROSS 8 JURISDICTIONS, AND THE DIRECTION IS LATE. Measured, not inferred; NOT fixed -- this needs Michael's decision.**
  **THE ASSIGNED QUESTION, ANSWERED FROM THE PRIMARY SOURCE.** Haw. R. Civ. P. 6(e), read in full from the Judiciary's own published rules: *"Whenever a party has the right or is required to do some act or take some proceedings within a prescribed period after **the service of a notice or other paper** upon the party and the notice or paper is served upon the party **by mail**, 2 days shall be added to the prescribed period."* **It is NOT gated on a service subsection the way Mont. R. Civ. P. 6(d) expressly is** -- Montana names `Rule 5(b)(2)(C),(D),(E),(F)`; Hawaii names no subsection at all. **But it reaches the same place by a different route**, because *"a notice or other paper"* is Rule 5 vocabulary and a summons and complaint go out under Rule 4 -- **the identical scope route this engine already applies to Massachusetts, West Virginia, North Carolina, Washington, New Jersey and Virginia**, written into `ma_rcp_6d`'s own comment. So Hawaii's Rule 36 row has the same limb mismatch Montana's does, and **unlike Montana's it CARRIES the extension**.
  **THEN THE SWEEP, BECAUSE ONE STATE'S BUG IS USUALLY THREE STATES' BUG.** Of 49 multi-trigger rows platform-wide, **15 carry a service extension**, and every one of those has a second limb that no mail rule reaches -- 11 run from **service of the summons/process**, and Maryland's 4 run from `date_initial_pleading_is_required`, which is not a service event at all.
  **MEASURED WITH THE ENGINE'S OWN METADATA, NOT BY COMPARING DATES, AND THE FIRST INSTRUMENT WAS WRONG.** A date-diff probe reported Hawaii as unaffected; it was a **collision** -- 45 days from 1 July lands on Saturday 15 August, and `add_to_period_then_roll` gives 17 August either way. Re-run against `service_extension.state`, Hawaii is `applied`, `days_added: 2`. **A probe that infers behaviour from an output can be fooled by arithmetic; the field that says what the engine DID cannot.** Corrected reading: **14 of 15 apply on the non-service limb** (the 15th, `md-rule-2-311b`, refuses for an unrelated `refused_missing_context`).
  **MISSISSIPPI IS NOT A READING -- IT IS FLATLY CONTRARY TO TEXT THIS REPO ALREADY QUOTES.** `ms_r_civ_p_6_e`'s own comment in the engine carries Rule 6(e) verbatim including its final sentence: *"**This subdivision does not apply to responses to service of summons under Rule 4.**"* The row adds 3 days on exactly that limb. **Alabama and Arkansas are wrong by principles this repo wrote down and applied elsewhere** -- `al_rcp_6_d`'s comment says *"6(d) reaches service made under RULE 5(b)(2) only; a summons and complaint go out under Rule 4"* and applies that to the ANSWER row while leaving the Rule 36 row's summons limb extended; `ar_rcp_6_d`'s says the carve-out *"is a property of the ROW"*. **Nebraska cites a subsection (§ 6-1105(b)(3)(C)) exactly like Montana. New Mexico cites Rule 1-005(C)(1)(e), the serve-and-file rule.**
  **DIRECTION: LATE, ON A SELF-EXECUTING RULE.** Ten of the fourteen are Rule 36 admissions rows, where the matter is admitted unless answered and Rule 36(b) makes the admission conclusively established. **Telling a defendant they have 2-3 more days than they do is how an admission is lost**, and this platform's standing rule since Kentucky is that a gap which can report LATE refuses rather than discloses.
  **WHY I STOPPED AT MEASURING.** The correct fix is **per-limb extension scoping** -- declared per row, not derived from the limb's event name, because a default computed from another field is the invisible-rule shape the `on_unconfirmed` work already rejected. That is a schema plus engine change touching **15 rows in 8 jurisdictions**, and declaring it correctly means reading 8 states' extension rules from primary sources (I have read only Montana and Hawaii; the other six rest on quotes captured at seed time). **The alternative -- stripping the extension from all 14, the Montana treatment -- is safe/EARLY and immediate but costs real coverage on the limb that legitimately earns the days.** Coverage-versus-safety across 8 jurisdictions is Michael's call, not mine, and a hasty 8-state edit is worse than a measured report.
  **VERIFIED: the sweep script, the 15-row table and the 14-of-15 result are reproducible from `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_*.json` and the live engine; NOTHING IN THE REPO WAS CHANGED BY THIS ENTRY.** 2026-09-02
