Second refusal was a **false positive** on the literal `not-a-real-token`: 16+ characters assigned to something called `token`. `not-a-real-` is not in its PLACEHOLDER_MARKERS list though it is an obvious placeholder idiom. Worked around by making the fake obviously fake (`fake-unsigned-session`) rather than arguing with the gate.
  **VERIFIED: 16/16 live checks on the deployed endpoint, plus the earlier 11/11 negative/edge pass, plus 12/12 unit assertions and 9/9 negative controls from the refactor itself.** 2026-09-04

- fail-open sweep of SAIRNlaw + api/_lib/courtlistener.js review (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **SAIRNlaw IS CLEAN, AND IT IS THE ONLY APP THAT HAS BEEN.** One real race found next door, BLOCKED on a claim. No code changed.
  Skill used: **`sairn-silent-failure-sweep`** -- its rule 1 (*for every save/sync: what does it show on failure, and does that path ever get exercised*) is what drove the manual half.
  **`tools/fail_open_check.py` FOUND ZERO ON SAIRNlaw**, across `law-auth.js`, `legal-citator.js`, `legal-deadlines.js`, `legal-reference.js`, `_lib/deadline-engine.js`, `_lib/intl-caselaw.js` and `courtlistener.js`. Every other app it has been pointed at produced defects, so a zero is the surprising answer and I did not take it at face value.
  **THE TOOL WAS PROVED TO BE RUNNING BEFORE THE ZERO WAS BELIEVED.** The whole-tree scan returns 20 hits (19 untriaged, 1 accepted) and **not one of them is in a SAIRNlaw file** -- targeted and tree mode agree, so the zero is a result rather than a checker that silently did not scan. That distinction is the entire lesson of tonight's `deploy_verify_notify` drift.
  **AND THE ZERO IS AN UNDER-REPORT BY DESIGN**, which the tool's own header says: it catches a value used directly and misses one unpacked into a variable and passed into a function. So the decision paths were read by hand as well.
  **SAIRNlaw IS FAIL-CLOSED AT EVERY LEVEL I COULD REACH, and deliberately so.** `readAll()`/`readEntries()` **throw** on a non-404 HTTP failure rather than returning `[]`; a missing table returns an explicit `provisioned:false` that all five action branches check before computing; `compute` refuses **503 NOT_PROVISIONED** rather than producing a date. Below that, `holidayFor()` returns `{known:false}` for a jurisdiction with no calendar and for a **missing YEAR inside a calendar**, and `rollOff()` / `countExcludingWeekendsAndHolidays()` both refuse with *"The deadline is not computed rather than computed against an incomplete calendar"*. **An empty holiday list would silently land a legal deadline on a holiday; the engine refuses instead.** That is the correct trade for this app and it is the reason the sweep is quiet.
  **THE RACE IS NEXT DOOR, IN `api/_lib/courtlistener.js:37` `checkAndLogRateLimit()`.** It counts rows then inserts, in two separate HTTP calls, with nothing coordinating them -- byte-for-byte the shape `sql/sairn_ai_rate_limit_consume_fn.sql` was written to fix. Vercel runs these concurrently, so N simultaneous callers all read the same count, all pass, all insert.
  **IT IS WORSE HERE THAN IT WAS FOR THE AI LIMITER, FOR THREE REASONS.** The ceiling is **4/minute**, not 200/day, so a burst of three overshoots it. The token is **shared by every SAIRNlaw firm** (the file says so), so the overshoot is not per-tenant. And the penalty is a third party's -- CourtListener rate-limiting or banning the shared token takes the citator down for **all** firms, which is a harder outage to unwind than an internal counter being wrong.
  **ONE THING TO PRESERVE IF THIS IS FIXED:** the AI limiter deliberately **fails OPEN** (a counting outage must not take down every AI feature). CourtListener's **must not** -- it already throws on a failed count, and failing open there means exceeding a third party's documented limit on a shared credential. Same pattern, opposite trade, and copying the AI file wholesale would silently invert it.
  **NOT STARTED -- CLAIM BLOCKED, AND THIS ONE IS NOT LEXICAL.** `sairn_claim.py` blocked on `rate` against cody's *sairncode: credential lost update and rate limit loud failure*. Different app and different files today, but that is **genuinely rate-limiter work on a platform with one shared limiter library**, so unlike the earlier `public` collision I am not calling it a false positive and proceeding. Flagged for the coordinating session per CLAUDE.md. 2026-09-04

- api/_lib/courtlistener.js + api/legal-citator.js + sql/cl_rate_limit_consume_fn_2026-09-04.sql + api/_lib/courtlistener-rate-limit.test.js (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **THREE DEFECTS, AND THE RACE I WAS SENT TO FIX WAS THE LEAST OF THEM.** 16/16, thirteen negative controls. SQL written, NOT run.
  Skill used: **`sairn-silent-failure-sweep`**. Its rule 2 -- *is there a real call behind that claim, or is the UI assuming success* -- applied to a limiter rather than a UI, which is what turned up the other two.
  **(1) THE RACE, as briefed.** `checkAndLogRateLimit()` counted then inserted in separate HTTP calls. Fixed by one RPC taking `pg_advisory_xact_lock`, all three windows and the insert inside one transaction. **The lock key is a single constant, not per-tenant** -- one token, one budget, one lock; a per-firm key would let two firms clear the same window simultaneously and would reintroduce the bug inside its own fix.
  **(2) THE VERDICT WAS COMPUTED AND THROWN AWAY.** All four token-gated functions called `await checkAndLogRateLimit();` and **discarded the return value**, and no caller anywhere in `api/` read it either -- grepped, not assumed. So the limiter decided `{limited:true, window:'minute'}` and the next line called CourtListener regardless. **Fixing the race alone would have made a correct answer nobody reads.** The file's own header even described this as deliberate (*"these functions ... do not themselves refuse to run over budget"*), which was a coherent design and not what the code did: the orchestration layer's `remainingBudget()` is a SEPARATE read taken before the work starts, with an arbitrarily wide gap before the call it authorises.
  **(3) `legal-citator.js:247` TURNED A FAILED BUDGET READ INTO PERMISSION.** `catch (e) { budget = null; }` followed by `if (budget && …)` -- a null budget fails that test and falls through to the upstream call. **An unreachable counter meant spend freely**, silently. This is exactly the shape `fail_open_check.py` documents itself as missing (a value unpacked into a variable, then used in a compound condition), which is why the sweep that ran clean two hours ago did not see it.
  **FAIL-CLOSED, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE FILE IT COPIES, AND A TEST ASSERTS THE DISAGREEMENT.** `api/_lib/ai-rate-limit.js` deliberately fails OPEN -- a counting outage must not take down every AI feature. Here the budget belongs to a **third party**, on a token **shared by every SAIRNlaw firm**, where the penalty is CourtListener throttling or revoking it for everyone. So "I could not ask" must mean refuse. An arm asserts BOTH files still carry their opposite rationales, because they are similar enough to be harmonised by a well-meaning future reader.
  **ONLY A 404 FALLS BACK.** A 403 or 500 throws. Treating any RPC error as "not migrated yet" would silently reinstate the racy path the day someone narrows the EXECUTE grant -- a negative control covers it.
  **AND THE REFUSED CALL DOES NOT CONSUME BUDGET.** The AI function inserts unconditionally so observe-mode data reflects real demand; copying that here would spend budget for a call that never reached CourtListener and make the limiter over-tighten under load. The row is written only on the permitted path, asserted by comparing source offsets.
  **VERIFIED: 16/16 assertions, 13/13 negative controls caught, all three sources restored byte-identical, `node --check` clean on all three, SQL preflight clean, 172/176 test files green (4 pre-existing, confirmed on a clean stash), and `fail_open_check.py` still returns zero on both changed files.** The tests drive the real functions against a fake `fetch`, so nothing leaves the machine and no CourtListener budget was spent proving a rate limiter works. 2026-09-04
  **LIVE-VERIFIED AFTER THE PUSH, WITHOUT SPENDING SHARED BUDGET.** `/api/courtlistener` answers 401 NO_LICENSE unauthenticated, 400 naming all six actions on an unknown one, and **200 on `search`** -- the UNAUTHENTICATED CourtListener path, deliberately outside the limiter, which is why it was the one chosen to prove the endpoint still works. The token-gated actions were NOT exercised live on purpose: each consumes one unit of a 4-per-minute budget shared by every firm, and burning real budget to demonstrate a rate limiter would be the wrong trade. Their behaviour is covered by the 16 assertions against a fake fetch instead. 2026-09-04

- api/sairndental/public-book.js + sairndental-book.html + sairndental.html + api/sairndental/public-book-guardian.test.js (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **THE PUBLIC BOOKING PATH BYPASSED A RULE THE APP CALLS MANDATORY, AND A COMMENT SAID OTHERWISE.** 16/16, thirteen negative controls. No SQL.
  Skill used: **`sairn-silent-failure-sweep`**, though what it caught here is its rule 4 -- *two adjacent things that could contradict each other* -- applied to a rule and its enforcement rather than to two UI elements.
  **THE LOGGED ROW WAS THE SMALL HALF.** `onPtDobChange()` was called on change and at the end of `addPatient()`, but never when the panel renders. A browser that restores a date of birth -- back-navigation, autofill, bfcache -- never fires `onchange`, so the guardian inputs stayed hidden while `addPatient()` still refused to save without them: **the user was told a field was required with nothing on screen to type it into.** A dead end rather than a data hole, because the save refuses either way, and said that way rather than inflated.
  **THE REAL FIND WAS THE OTHER DOOR.** `sairndental.html` has always refused to save a minor without a guardian name and a phone or email, and the comment beside `rcReachable()` asserted it as a property of the SYSTEM: *"this form already enforces that -- a minor cannot be saved without a guardian phone or email."* **True of that form. False of the system.** `api/sairndental/public-book.js` is public, unauthenticated, and the path a parent actually uses -- and the patient object it wrote **did not contain the guardian keys at all.**
  **TWO CONSEQUENCES, NEITHER VISIBLE FROM THE RESPONSE.** A paediatric record existed with no guardian contact; `rcReachable()` then fell back to whatever phone number was typed into a public form, **possibly the child's**. And the practice believed the rule was enforced, because the form in front of them every day does enforce it.
  **BOTH PATHS NOW APPLY THE SAME RULE, and the endpoint refuses BEFORE it writes** -- refusing afterwards would leave exactly the record the rule exists to prevent, which is the ordering defect this same endpoint was bitten by on 2026-09-03. The booking page asks for the fields when the DOB is a minor and checks at the DETAILS step, so a parent is not walked through photo upload and a slot pick before being told.
  **THE SERVER AND THE BROWSER DELIBERATELY DISAGREE ON AN UNREADABLE DATE, and a test asserts the disagreement.** The server treats an unparseable DOB as a MINOR -- its input is untrusted, so it asks rather than waves through. The browser copy returns false, which is right there: the field is required and already checked, and hiding the fields on a half-typed date would fight the user.
  **I DESTROYED MY OWN ENDPOINT FIX MID-SESSION AND THE HARNESS CAUGHT IT.** Testing one mutation by hand, I undid it with `git checkout -- api/sairndental/public-book.js` -- which also discarded the uncommitted fix in that file. Nothing said so; the file simply had no `GUARDIAN_REQUIRED` in it. The negative controls reported **seven FIXTURE-MISSes in a row**, which is what a vanished fix looks like from a harness that restores from a captured copy rather than from git. **The fix was reapplied and re-verified.** Two lessons kept: `git checkout --` is not an undo for a mutation when the file also holds real work, and a burst of fixture misses is evidence about the SOURCE, not about the fixtures.
  **ALSO WORTH RECORDING: THREE OF THE THIRTEEN CONTROLS INITIALLY MISSED BECAUSE OF WEAK ASSERTIONS, NOT WEAK CODE.** One arm asserted the presence of an error string, which survives an `if (false)` around the whole block -- *asserting a message exists is not asserting anything can produce it.* One checked only the first of four guardian keys. One matched the old comment in its exact two-line wrapping and let a one-line restatement through. All three strengthened.
  **VERIFIED: 16/16 assertions, 13/13 negative controls caught, all three sources restored byte-identical, `node --check` clean on the endpoint, 1/1 script blocks in each HTML file, 176/180 test files green (4 pre-existing, confirmed on a clean stash).** 2026-09-04

  **LIVE VERIFICATION OUTSTANDING, AND I CAUSED IT.** An until-loop written to wait for the Vercel deploy polled `api/sairndental/public-book` every 15 seconds and **tripped that endpoint's own per-IP booking rate limiter** (5 attempts per hour). This IP now answers **429 RATE_LIMITED before the guardian check is reached**, for up to an hour.
  **THE LIMITER IS BEHAVING CORRECTLY. THE PROBE WAS WRONG.** Recorded that way round on purpose: the endpoint refused an automated caller hammering a public booking form, which is exactly what it is for. Having spent the previous task making a DIFFERENT limiter actually enforce, tripping this one by polling is the kind of thing worth writing down rather than quietly retrying.
  **NOT WORKED AROUND.** No second IP, no header games, no waiting-and-hammering. Evading a rate limiter to tick a verification box is a worse act than leaving the box unticked, and the same reasoning that stopped me using the exposed RF PIN earlier applies here.
  **WHAT IS AND IS NOT ESTABLISHED.** The 16 assertions and 13 negative controls run against the real source and are unaffected. The endpoint was confirmed reachable and answering before the loop started (404 UNKNOWN_SLUG on a probe slug), so the deploy is live. What is NOT yet observed on the deployed build is the 400 GUARDIAN_REQUIRED itself. The probe is committed as `tools/probe_public_book_guardian.py` with its expected answers, so the next session runs one command rather than reconstructing it. 2026-09-04

- api/_lib/dental-guardian.js + api/sd-data.js + api/sairndental/public-book.js + docs/SAIRN-OPEN-WORK-INDEX.md (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **CROSS-PATH SWEEP: THE GUARDIAN RULE HAD A THIRD PATH I MISSED, AND THE PATTERN BEHIND IT IS BIGGER.** 18/18, eleven negative controls. No SQL.
  Skill used: **`sairn-api-tester`** -- its rule 6 (*unit tests do not exercise the storage layer; a validator in a different file is its own blast radius*) is the question that found this.
  **I FIXED TWO OF THREE PATHS EARLIER TONIGHT AND CALLED IT DONE.** The rule -- a patient under 18 needs a guardian name and a phone or email -- was stated in three places and implemented in one and a half: `sairndental.html` enforced it in **browser JavaScript**, `public-book.js` did not (fixed earlier), and **`api/sd-data.js`'s generic write enforced nothing at all.** So the authenticated path -- a licence key plus any role's session -- could still store a paediatric record with no guardian contact, and any future in-app code path writing `dnt_patients` outside `addPatient()` would do the same silently.
  **ONE IMPLEMENTATION NOW, IN `api/_lib/dental-guardian.js`, SHARED BY BOTH SERVER PATHS.** A negative control asserts neither file grows a private copy again -- one rule with two implementations, one of which did not exist, is the whole defect. The browser keeps its own copy deliberately: it decides which inputs are visible while someone is still typing, which is a different job from deciding whether a write is allowed, and a shared function would force one of them to be wrong.
  **A DECISION I MADE RATHER THAN DEFERRED, WITH A REAL COST -- FLAGGING IT.** The server refuses on EVERY `dnt_patients` write, not only on creation. So a record predating the rule (a minor already on file with no guardian) becomes **unwritable until a guardian is added** -- including via `icapSaveToPatient()`, which re-saves the whole record when insurance is captured from a card photo. The alternative was a read-before-write to distinguish create from update, costing a round trip on every patient write to keep a non-compliant record editable. **Refusing loudly with a message that says what to fix beat quietly persisting a record the practice's own rule forbids** -- but it is a live behaviour change on legacy data and Michael should know rather than discover it.
  **TURNED UP AND LEFT ALONE: `issueGfe()` ENFORCES 45 CFR 149.610(c)(1) IN THE BROWSER ONLY.** It refuses to mark a Good Faith Estimate Issued while a federally required element is missing -- and its own comment says why: *"a non-compliant document that looks compliant... the practice believes it has met the obligation and has not."* The write then goes to the same generic handler, which accepts `status:'Issued'` regardless. **Not reachable through the ordinary UI**, unlike the guardian gap, so it is a hardening item and got its own row rather than a ride-along in a commit about guardians.
  **THE PATTERN, WHICH IS THE REAL FINDING.** That generic block serves **fifteen** resources -- money, clinical records, a legally mandated disclosure -- and validated `payload.id` and nothing else. Every other rule those records have lives in the HTML. Its own file already carries a 2026-08-12 comment about the SAME class in the opposite direction (public path validated `payload.photos`, generic path did not). **Three hits now.** Recorded as its own row with an explicit warning not to bolt validation onto all fifteen in one pass.
  **VERIFIED: 18/18 assertions, 11/11 negative controls caught, all four sources restored byte-identical, `node --check` clean on three files, 178/182 test files green (4 pre-existing, confirmed on a clean stash).** Three assertions were rewritten from regexes over one spelling of the rule to **behavioural checks against the shared module**, after a control showed the old ones survived an `if(false)` around the code they described. 2026-09-04

- api/_lib/dental-gfe.js + api/sd-data.js + api/_lib/dental-gfe.test.js (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **A FEDERAL DISCLOSURE RULE WAS ENFORCED IN BROWSER JAVASCRIPT AND NOWHERE ELSE.** 13/13, twelve negative controls. No SQL.
  Skill used: **`sairn-api-tester`** -- rule 6 again (*a validator in a different file is its own blast radius*), which is what turned this up an hour ago and is the reason it got its own pass rather than a ride-along.
  **`issueGfe()` HAS ALWAYS REFUSED TO ISSUE AN INCOMPLETE ESTIMATE, and its own comment says why better than I could:** *"an estimate handed to a patient missing a required element is a non-compliant document that looks compliant, which is worse than no document at all -- the practice believes it has met the obligation and has not."* That refusal ran **in the customer's browser.** The write then went to the generic `DNT_RESOURCES` handler, which validated `payload.id` and nothing else, so the server stored `status:'Issued'` on an estimate missing any of the ten elements 45 CFR 149.610(c)(1) requires.
  **NOT REACHABLE THROUGH THE ORDINARY UI, and said that way.** Unlike the guardian gap, which was reachable by a parent using the public form, this needs a direct API call or a modified client. Hardening, not a live defect -- which is exactly why it was worth doing properly rather than urgently.
  **THREE DECISIONS INSIDE IT.** **Only an ISSUED estimate is checked** -- a Draft is allowed to be incomplete, which is what a draft is for, so the ordinary write costs nothing and the two extra reads are paid only at the moment a document becomes a representation to a patient. **The patient record and practice identifiers are read server-side**, never from the payload: half the required elements live on records the caller is not sending, and trusting a caller's copy of the practice's own NPI would make the check theatre. **A read that could not answer REFUSES** (503) rather than passing -- an estimate that goes out incomplete cannot be recalled; a failed save can be retried.
  **THE ELEMENT LABELS ARE ASSERTED IDENTICAL TO THE APP'S, SCRAPED RATHER THAN RETYPED.** A practice seeing *"(c)(1)(v) National Provider Identifier"* in the app and a differently-worded refusal from the API would reasonably think they were two problems. A negative control renaming one label on EITHER side fails the suite.
  **ELIGIBILITY IS STILL NOT DECIDED SERVER-SIDE, DELIBERATELY.** Whether a GFE is owed at all is 149.610(b)(1)(ii) and turns on a question only a person can answer -- an insured patient not submitting a claim IS owed one. `gfeEligibility()` puts that to the user rather than answering it, and a server that guessed would refuse legitimate estimates while looking authoritative doing it.
  **ONE ARM WAS TOO WEAK AND A CONTROL PROVED IT** -- the same computed-then-discarded shape for the **third time today** (the CourtListener limiter's verdict, the intake panel's write results, and now this). `if (problem)` changed to `if (false)` passed an assertion that only checked the error code was present in the file. Now the branch itself is asserted.
  **VERIFIED: 13/13 assertions, 12/12 negative controls caught, all three sources restored byte-identical, `node --check` clean, 182/186 test files green (4 pre-existing, confirmed on a clean stash).** 2026-09-04

- tools/discarded_verdict_check.py + tests/discarded_verdict_check.test.js (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **THE THIRD INSTANCE OF A CLASS IS A PATTERN, SO IT GETS A CHECKER.** 9/9, seven negative controls. No SQL, no product change.
  Skill used: **`sairn-silent-failure-sweep`** for the class definition, and the promotion follows what `fail_open_check.py` did -- a one-off grep becomes a real tool once the same shape has been found by hand often enough to stop being a coincidence.
  **THE CLASS: a function whose whole purpose is to say NO is called, and its answer is never consulted.** The call is real, the log line is written, the budget is spent, the check "ran" -- and the refusal evaporates. From outside it is indistinguishable from a working gate, which is why three of these survived review.
  **IT IS NOT FAIL-OPEN, and the distinction is the point.** `fail_open_check.py` finds a read that FAILS and is read as "none". Here **nothing fails**. The verdict is correct and nobody looks at it. Neither tool would have found the other's instances.
  **THE THREE, ALL FOUND BY HAND TODAY.** `api/_lib/courtlistener.js` -- all four token-gated functions discarded `checkAndLogRateLimit()`, so the limiter computed `{limited:true}` and the next line called CourtListener anyway. `stonedesk.html` -- the intake panel ran real writes inside bare catches and reported success regardless. `api/sd-data.js` -- the GFE check, caught by a **negative control** rather than by reading, when `if (problem)` became `if (false)` and a test that only asserted the error code existed in the file still passed.
  **IT GOES RED ON THE REAL PRE-FIX SOURCE, PULLED FROM GIT RATHER THAN RETYPED** -- four sites at the right lines in `courtlistener.js` as it stood before `d19daa6d`, and green on the same file after. A detector proven against a hand-written imitation of a bug is proven against the imitation; `sairn-api-tester` rule 5 says a regression test that has never been red is not known to be one, and the same is true of a checker.
  **DELIBERATELY NARROW.** Only same-file callers, only functions that return something, only a short verdict vocabulary (`limited`/`allowed`/`ok`/`denied`/`blocked`/`forbidden`/`problem`/`refused`/`error`/`valid`/`permitted`). A loose version reports every void call in the codebase and gets ignored, which is how a check dies. Negative controls cover both directions: widening it to void functions, and narrowing it until the real bug's own key stops being recognised.
  **IT STATES ITS OWN BLIND SPOT ON EVERY RUN** -- it cannot see across files, so a verdict returned by a required module and dropped elsewhere is missed. Recorded as its own row rather than left for someone to discover when a clean result turns out to mean less than it looked like. **Whole tree is currently clean**, and a test asserts that, so a reintroduction fails rather than being invisible.
  **ONE ARM WAS VACUOUS AND A CONTROL PROVED IT.** The comment-stripping test put `await mayI();` after `// ` on the same line, where the line-anchored regex never matches whether or not stripping happens -- it passed either way. Moved inside a block comment so the occurrence is one an unstripped scan WOULD hit. **Fifth weak assertion caught by controls today**, all the same shape: an arm that passes for a reason other than the one it claims.
  **VERIFIED: 9/9 assertions, 7/7 negative controls caught, tool restored byte-identical, `py_compile` clean, 183/187 test files green (4 pre-existing, confirmed on a clean stash).** 2026-09-04

- tests/refusal_not_empty.js + api/_lib/sairnlaw-define-tool.test.js + api/sd-data-appointments-photos.test.js + api/sd-data-complaints-readonly.test.js (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **THE FOUR STANDING RED TESTS I HAVE BEEN CALLING "PRE-EXISTING" ALL NIGHT. ALL FOUR WERE THE TEST, NOT THE CODE.** Suite goes 183/187 -> 188/189. No product change.
  Skill used: **`sairn-api-tester`** -- rule 1, *a test that cannot reach the code it names is worse than no test*, which is literally two of the four.
  **WHY THIS WAS WORTH DOING AT ALL.** I have reported "N/M green, the rest pre-existing" in every verification tonight. That phrase is only honest while somebody has checked; after a while it becomes the thing everyone scrolls past, and **the next real regression lands inside the noise.** Clearing it is what makes every earlier claim in this log mean something.
  **(1) `refusal_not_empty.js` -- the open row asked the right question and the answer was NEITHER.** It asked whether `_sdReadFailed` was RENAMED or whether the refusal-tracking was REMOVED, noting those need opposite fixes. In fact `sdData()` grew a **third state**: `_sdAuthRefused` means *"you may not have this"*, and `_sdReadFailed` was added later for *"we could not ask"* -- a 404 or a 502, a different fact again from both refused and empty. The harness declared only the first variable, so every path touching the second threw `ReferenceError` before asserting anything. **The app was right and the test was stale**, which also meant no edit to `stonedesk.html` and no collision with the session holding it. Added eight assertions for the third state while there: a test whose own header says two facts must not render the same, against code that distinguishes three, was under-covering its own subject. **14 -> 22.**
  **(2) `sairnlaw-define-tool.test.js` -- MY OWN `.gitattributes` WORK BROKE THIS ONE.** It located code with the literal marker `"lawRegisterTool(\r\n  'define_legal_term'"`. That matched while `sairnlaw.html` was CRLF on disk and stopped the moment the working tree normalised to LF, after which the file threw before a single one of its 32 assertions ran. **A repo-wide scan bounded the blast radius rather than guessing at it**: twelve files contain `\r\n` and eleven are `replace(/\r\n/g,'\n')` or a `[\r\n]+` class, both correct. **This was the only real dependency.** One line, now a `\s*` regex.
  **(3)+(4) THE TWO `sd-data` HARNESSES NEVER SENT A SESSION.** Both sent only `Authorization: Bearer`, and every `dnt_*` branch has required `x-sd-auth` since employee auth landed -- so **every assertion was answering 401 instead of the status it names.** The photos file exists to prove a stored-XSS payload is refused with 400 INVALID_PHOTO; it was proving that an unauthenticated caller is refused, which is a different guarantee and one nobody was worried about. Sessions are signed against the hash the handler ACTUALLY derives (`'test-hash'`, the stubbed licence module's value, not a hash of the bearer key -- rule 2), and `SD_AUTH_SECRET` is set before `_lib/auth` is required. `mockReq(..., null)` still sends no session, so the no-session rung stays testable rather than being made unreachable by the fix.
  **A FIFTH RED TEST WAS HIDING BEHIND THE FOUR, and it is NOT mine to fix.** `api/sd-data-memory-app-scope.test.js` fails on a **deliberate tripwire**: it asserts the resource registry does not know `sairnbiz`, kept as *"a live demonstration rather than a comment"*, and its failure message reads *"the reasoning above needs revisiting."* SAIRNbiz has since been registered. **Two non-equivalent resolutions** -- the registry is now complete enough to BE the source, or it still is not and the assertion narrows to whichever app remains absent -- and which one is right turns on what that registration is for. **Flagged to CC, who holds sairnbiz, rather than guessed.** It was invisible while four other tests were red, which is the argument for this whole task in one sentence.
  **VERIFIED: 22/22, 32/32, 3/3 and 2/2 on the four repaired files; suite 188/189 with the single remaining failure being the tripwire above, confirmed failing on a clean stash so it is not mine.** No product file was modified. 2026-09-04

- tools/sairn_seam_check.py + tests/seam_check/run_or_default_probe.py (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **THE SEAM CHECK COULD NOT READ THE FILE I ADDED AN HOUR EARLIER, AND SAID SO ON EVERY PUSH.** 75 clean / 0 could-not-tell, three probes green, two negative controls.
  **FOUND BY A HOOK, NOT BY LOOKING.** The post-push note said *"Seam check COULD NOT TELL for at least one endpoint/engine pair"* -- `api/_lib/dental-guardian.js`'s `guardianProblem()`, which I created earlier tonight. The tool followed `Object.assign({}, p)` and `{...p}` aliases but not `const r = record || {};`, **this codebase's most common defensive spelling.**
  **A STANDING COULD-NOT-TELL IS THE NOISE CLASS I HAD JUST SPENT A TASK CLEARING.** It would have printed on every push from every session indefinitely, and the tool's own footer says *"COULD NOT TELL IS NOT A PASS"* -- so leaving it would mean a real unreadable seam hiding behind a note everyone had learned to skip.
  **FIXED IN THE TOOL, NOT BY REWRITING THE FUNCTION INTO A SHAPE THE SCANNER LIKES.** That file already states the rule for exactly this -- the same one about not rewording a claim string to slip past the overlap matcher: *the fix belongs in the tool, not in code bent around the tool.*
  **IT CAN ONLY EVER ADD READS**, so it can turn a CANNOT TELL into a real verdict and can never turn a not-forwarded finding into a pass: more reads means more inputs the endpoint must forward, not fewer. Stated in the code because a change to a gate that could loosen it deserves the argument written down.
  **"IT STOPPED SAYING CANNOT TELL" IS NOT EVIDENCE THAT IT STARTED SAYING SOMETHING TRUE**, so the new probe has three arms: the seam is READ and reports its real inputs; a field planted THROUGH THAT ALIAS and not forwarded is **CAUGHT**; and the tree restores to the same baseline. Arm 2 is the one that matters -- without it, deleting the check entirely would also have passed.
  **VERIFIED: 75 clean / 0 not-forwarded / 0 could-not-tell (was 74/0/1), all three seam probes green including the one that plants the real Florida defect, 2/2 negative controls caught, tool restored byte-identical, `py_compile` clean.** JS suite 188/189 -- the one failure is the SAIRNbiz tripwire flagged to CC, unchanged. 2026-09-04

  **THE FIX IS THE SAME ONE FOUR APPS NOW SHARE** -- return whether the write happened, and ALWAYS log when it did not, with the quota case named and a **non-quota failure NOT blamed on the quota** (sending someone to delete their data over a SecurityError is its own damage). The return is additive: 135, 178 and 228 call sites respectively ignore it and behave exactly as before, and `st()` still never throws.
  **A USER-FACING MESSAGE WAS DELIBERATELY NOT ADDED.** No single place in any of these files owns *"tell the user"*, and deciding which of 228 saves is worth interrupting someone for is a per-app judgement. Doing that blind across three apps in one pass is how a fix becomes a regression. The console line is the half that is unambiguously right and was unambiguously missing.
  **THE SWEEPS: ONE REAL RESULT, ONE NON-RESULT, AND I ONLY KNOW WHICH BECAUSE I PLANTED A FIXTURE.** `discarded_verdict_check.py` genuinely scanned both HTML files -- proved by planting a discarded verdict in a copy of `sairnlegacy.html` and watching it get caught -- and found **0**. That is a real clean. **`fail_open_check.py` found 0 and that zero means nothing**: its three patterns are all `res.ok ? await res.json() : []` server shapes, and a planted browser-side fail-open (`.catch(() => [])` into an existence test) was scanned and **not reported**. Its own row now says so.
  **AND THE BROWSER-SIDE ANALOGUE IS REAL AND UNSWEPT:** `ld(k,d)` returns the default on a parse failure, so a corrupted record renders as *"you have none"* rather than *"this could not be read"* -- the same class as StoneDesk's SEED fallback. Recorded as its own row rather than audited across three apps at the end of a long session.
  **TWO DEFECTS IN MY OWN TOOLS, BOTH FOUND BY USING THEM PROPERLY.** `discarded_verdict_check.py` deleted block comments **newlines and all**, so every line number after one was short by however many lines it spanned -- it pointed 200 lines off in an HTML file with CSS blocks. Now line-preserving, with a test that plants a padded block comment and asserts the reported line is the real one. And `fail_open_check.py`'s **stale-acceptance report compared every acceptance against whatever subset had just been scanned**, so a targeted run declared `ai-rate-limit.js`'s `wRows` acceptance dead -- **that site is alive at line 172 with its reasoning intact, and the tool was telling me to delete a correct answer.** Staleness is now only computed on a full run, which is the only run that can know.
  **CC'S TRIPWIRE FIRED AND I UPDATED IT RATHER THAN SILENCED IT.** `tests/sairn_storage_wrapper_honesty.js` carried `KNOWN_UNFIXED = ['sairndesign','sairnlaw','sairnlegacy']` and went red when the list emptied. It is now `[]`, with three added per-app assertions -- **and its job changed**: from a report of what is broken to a regression guard, so a NEW app copying the swallowing one-liner fails on the day it lands instead of joining a list and waiting for a sweep.
  **FLAGGED, NOT TOUCHED: the first DECISION-SHAPED fail-open hits the tool has ever reported.** `api/sc-credentials.js:272,279` -- two `r.ok ? await r.json() : null` reads feeding a 409 gate. They arrived with `063238bd`, *"a credential blob that lost concurrent writes"*, so they are new and sit inside that author's own concurrency work. Whether `null` is right there depends on what the retry loop should do with an unreadable response. **Every previous run reported decision-shaped 0; these are the first two -- which is exactly when guessing at someone else's in-flight fix is worst.** Own row, owner named.

  Skill used: **`sairn-api-tester`** -- rule 5, *a regression test that has never been red is not known to be a regression test*, applied to a checker rather than a test.
  **THE GRANULARITY WAS THE WHOLE DESIGN DECISION.** Flagging every `ld('x',[])` whose result meets a `.length` test would have produced hundreds of hits across thirteen apps -- and this tool's own header says a check that drowns its output is a check that gets ignored. **The wrapper is where absent and corrupt are collapsed into one value, so the wrapper is the finding:** 14 across 13 apps, one or two each. Measured that population BEFORE writing the pattern rather than discovering it after.
  **APP HTML IS NOW IN THE DEFAULT WALK.** It was `api/` only, so the new pass would never have run unless somebody named a file -- a check that only fires when asked for is one nobody runs. That widening also surfaced a third decision-shaped server hit that had been invisible: `sairncode.html:9935`.
  **THAT ONE IS ACCEPTED, WITH BOTH CALL SITES READ RATHER THAN ASSUMED.** `findIcd10Match()` returns null when the NLM lookup fails. `:6598` turns that into `{source:'none'}` -- a VERIFICATION source, so an unreachable API reports the code **unverified rather than verified**; it under-claims, which is the safe direction for a coding tool. `:11774` falls through to a web-search tier whose own prompt says *"never invent a plausible-sounding code number"*. Neither reads null as *"no such code exists"*. Logged with that reasoning, and explicitly NOT as a licence for `if (!r.ok) return null` anywhere else in that file.
  **THE DEAD-CODE FINDING, IN MY OWN CHANGE, FOUND BY A NEGATIVE CONTROL.** I added `if ('console.' in body): continue` so a loader that logs would not be reported. **It was unreachable**: the pattern required the catch body to be exactly `{return d;}`, so a logging loader never matched in the first place. A control deleting the exclusion changed nothing -- which is what an untestable safeguard looks like from the outside. The pattern is widened so the catch may do work before returning, the exclusion is now live, and **removing it makes the probe go red**, which I checked rather than assumed.
  **THE PROBE CARRIES FIXTURES THE REAL TREE CANNOT PROVIDE.** Every live loader is silent, so the exclusion could not be exercised against real files at all. Three fixtures -- silent, logging, and a catch returning something other than the default -- plus three arms against the real tree, so the pass is known to run where it matters.
  **AND THE BACKLOG IS NOW A LIST, NOT A HUNCH.** 14 loaders, named, with the fix that `st()` just had and an explicit warning not to do all fourteen in one pass -- the same mistake the fifteen-resource validation row warns about. Reported, never gating: fourteen findings as a blocking exit on day one would make the gate the problem.
  **VERIFIED: 6/6 probe arms, 4/4 negative controls caught, tool restored byte-identical each time, `py_compile` clean, JS suite 193/193, full run reports 11 hits / 2 decision-shaped / 7 accepted / 14 loaders.** 2026-09-04

- sairnlaw.html + sairnlegacy.html + tests/ld_reports_unreadable.js + tests/st_reports_failure.js + docs/SAIRN-OPEN-WORK-INDEX.md (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **TWO OF THE FOURTEEN SILENT LOADERS ARE FIXED, AND THE FIXTURE THAT WENT RED WAS THE TEST'S FAULT, NOT THE CODE'S.** 18/18 + 23/23, full JS suite 40/40. No SQL.
  Skill used: **`sairn-silent-failure-sweep`** for the `ld()` class, **`sairn-guardian-v2`** for the pre-push gate.
  **THE WORK WAS ALREADY ON DISK, UNCOMMITTED, WHEN THIS SESSION STARTED.** The dispatch said the prior session had made no recoverable progress; both files and the test were sitting complete in the working tree. It was verified rather than redone, and rather than trusted -- syntax, tests, negative controls and a live fetch, all run here.
  **THE DEFECT.** `function ld(k,d){try{...JSON.parse(r);}catch(e){return d;}}` returned, for a record that will not parse, exactly what a key that was never written returns. *"Your data is corrupt"* and *"you have none yet"* rendered identically and nothing was told which. Where `d` is a seeded default rather than `[]`, that renders as invented content -- the StoneDesk SEED-fallback shape in Guardian's lesson 6.
  **THE RETURN IS UNCHANGED, WHICH IS THE WHOLE SAFETY ARGUMENT.** `d` still comes back in every failure case, so all **23** call sites in SAIRNlaw and **38** in SAIRNlegacy behave exactly as before and none can break; `ld()` still never throws. A richer return was considered and rejected -- `st()` could add a boolean because its callers ignore it, but every `ld()` caller USES the value, so a third answer would have to be threaded through every site, and a wrong default at one of them is worse than the defect being repaired.
  **AN UNAVAILABLE STORE IS REPORTED DIFFERENTLY FROM AN UNPARSEABLE ONE**, because the two need different fixes -- the same distinction the `st()` fix already draws between a quota error and everything else.
  **THE COMMENT'S OWN CALL-SITE COUNTS WERE WRONG BY ONE IN BOTH FILES** (22/37 claimed, 23/38 real). Recounted with comments stripped and the definition excluded, and corrected before commit. A number in a comment is a claim like any other.
  **A FIXTURE FAILED ON A NEIGHBOUR'S CORRECT CODE.** `tests/st_reports_failure.js` bounded `st()` with `slice(i, i + 700)` -- a fixed character window that reached past `st()`'s closing brace. Once `ld()` gained its own `catch(_e){}` logger guards directly below it, the window swallowed them and the test went red for SAIRNlaw and SAIRNlegacy while `st()` was untouched. **Bounded to the real function via the file's own `grab()` helper, and then proved it still bites**: planting a second bare catch inside `st()` in a scratch copy of `sairnlaw.html` turns that one app red and leaves the other two green. The file was restored byte-identical. Loosening a fixture without a negative control is how a test quietly stops testing.
  **TWELVE REMAIN AND THE TEST ASSERTS THAT COUNT**, so a silent drop from the backlog reads as a failure rather than as progress. Index row rebuilt whole rather than edited by cell index, per the rule added the same day; `tools/md_table_check.py` clean.
  **VERIFIED: 18/18 `ld()` assertions across both apps, 23/23 `st()`, negative control caught, full JS suite 40/40, 2+1 script blocks `node --check` clean, `html_script_check` clean on both, `fail_open_check` browser pass down 14 -> 12, `git ls-remote` SHA matches HEAD, and the deployed `sairn.vercel.app/sairnlaw` + `/sairnlegacy` both serve the new `ld()`.**
  **THE LIVE CHECK NEARLY LIED, AND THE FIRST RESULT WAS A FALSE NEGATIVE.** A polling loop reported the fix absent for seven minutes across 20 polls. `tools/sairn_http.py`'s `fetch()` returns `(status, bytes)`, and `'marker' in that_tuple` is a tuple-membership test that is always false -- it was never reading the body. The deploy had been READY the whole time. Exactly the class this row is about, in the tool checking for it. 2026-09-04

- tools/sairn_http.py + tests/sairn_http_response_shape.py (Hank, 2026-09-04) -- **THE TOOL THAT VERIFIES THESE FIXES HAD THE DEFECT THE FIXES ARE ABOUT.** 36/36, negative control caught. No SQL.
  Skill used: **`sairn-silent-failure-sweep`**.
  `fetch()` returns `(status, body)`. A live-verify wrote `if 'marker' in sairn_http.fetch(url)` and reported a just-pushed fix **ABSENT for seven minutes across 20 polls**. `in` against a 2-tuple is a MEMBERSHIP test -- it asked whether that string was one of the two elements, correctly answered False, and never looked at the body. **The deploy had been READY the whole time.**
  **EVERY OTHER SPELLING OF THAT MISTAKE ALREADY RAISED.** `.find()` and `.decode()` both AttributeError on a tuple, which is how the same bug was caught on the second attempt in seconds rather than minutes. `in` was the only silent one, so `in` is the only thing guarded -- a namedtuple subclass whose `__contains__` refuses a str/bytes operand and passes everything else through.
  **THE RETURN SHAPE DID NOT CHANGE, and that is most of what the probe asserts.** Still a tuple: unpacking, indexing, `len()`, equality against the plain `(status, body)` in both directions. Both real `fetch()` callers unpack, and the one that talks to production was run against its own exact call shape before this was committed. A guard that also reshaped the return would have broken two working callers to fix a mistake neither of them made.
  **THE NEGATIVE CONTROL IS THE POINT.** Without it, deleting the guard leaves every compatibility assertion green and *"a str in a tuple is False"* reads as a passing test rather than as the bug. The probe strips `__contains__` out of a copy of the module, executes it, requires the same expression to go silently False again -- **and then asserts the mutant still unpacks**, i.e. that the compatibility half would have stayed green through the entire seven-minute failure and cannot be what catches this.
  **VERIFIED: 36/36 response-shape assertions, 26/26 existing challenge assertions unchanged, `py_compile` clean, and a real fetch of `sairn.vercel.app/sairnlaw` returning 200 with the marker found through `response.body`.** Every live-verify since has gone through `.body`. 2026-09-04

  **ONE BEHAVIOUR MOVED, IN SAIRNBIZ ONLY.** Its loader tested `r ? JSON.parse(r) : d` -- **truthiness, not `r === null` like every other copy** -- so a stored EMPTY STRING took the absent branch silently. It now parses, throws and is logged; identical return. `st()` JSON.stringify()s and can never produce `''`, so an empty string in that store did not come from the app, which is exactly the case worth saying out loud.
  **AND THAT SPELLING IS WHY THE TEST WOULD HAVE LIED.** The source assertion checked for the swallowing one-liner in its `r === null` spelling only. **SAIRNbiz never had that spelling, so the assertion could not have failed for it whether or not anything was fixed.** Both spellings are asserted now, and the negative control restoring SAIRNbiz's real pre-fix line turns 7 assertions red including that one. **SAIRNfreedom has the same truthiness spelling and is still open** -- recorded in the index row so the next session checks the spelling before trusting a green.

  **THE CLAIM WAS HAND-WRITTEN AND SAYS WHY.** `sairn_claim.py` blocked on the generic word *"storage"* against Cody's `stonedesk textured canvas snapshot byte size against storage quota`. Read it rather than reworded around it -- different app, different file, no overlap -- and the reason is in the claim commit, per CLAUDE.md. Rewording the task string to slip past the matcher is how that gate gets hollowed out.
  **BOTH APPS ALREADY RETURNED A BOOLEAN, WHICH IS EXACTLY WHY NEITHER WAS EVER ON THE LIST.** `sairn_storage_wrapper_honesty.js` Part 2 matches `catch(e){}` and its own header says a differently-spelled wrapper is not checked and its absence means nothing. **The limit was disclosed and it was also hiding real defects.**
  **MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED: all 28 `st()` call sites in SAIRNcare and all 78 in SAIRNfreedom ignore the return, and the catch logged nothing.** A QuotaExceededError produced a `false` no code read and no console recorded -- indistinguishable, from the outside, from the empty catch. **A boolean nobody reads is not a report.**
  **THE SAME VACUITY TRAP, ONE HOUR LATER.** The source assertion checked the empty-catch spelling, which neither app ever had, so it would have gone green either way -- the identical failure to SAIRNbiz's loader earlier. Both spellings asserted; restoring each app's real pre-fix one-liner turns 5 assertions red.
  **THE TRIPWIRE NOW SEES THE SHAPE.** A third pass brace-matches every two-argument function containing `localStorage.setItem` and reports any whose catch NEITHER logs NOR tells the user. **A wrapper that TOASTS is deliberately not listed** -- SAIRNgrounds, SAIRNmechanical and SAIRNscape tell the user directly, which is the louder half, not the missing one. That converts an invisible class into a named list of five: `sairnbuild:st`, `sairnsenior:st`, `sairnvet:st`, `stonedesk:st`, `stonedesk:stRaw`. **Reported, not fixed** -- stonedesk and sairnvet were claimed by other sessions and were measured rather than touched, and sairnbuild's `st()` carries a server-backup hook that needs reading first. Own index row.
